# Decisions so far, and why

All work is ILLUSTRATIVE / SYNTHETIC until real client data and expert review exist.

## Deferred polish (a running list, revisited later rather than per stage)
Small improvements that were deliberately left out so the stages stay focused. Add to this list; do not do them inside a stage unless asked.

| Item | Where it came from | Status |
|---|---|---|
| Expose the Stage 2 data settings in the setup app (delimiter, decimal mark, sheet, header row, missing-value markers, date format and language, per-column units, assumed units, composition and weight tolerance, hardness fixed unit, provenance, joins). Today they can only be edited in the setup file | Stage 2a; decided 2026-10-11 to keep the Data screen as it is | deferred |
| A "Check this file" button on the setup app's Data screen that reads the chosen file and shows the column names, detected separator and a few messages | the Stage 2 plan (optional step) | deferred |
| Expose the Stage 3a `data.validation` section on the setup app's Data screen (responses, inputs, calculated-from list, left-out columns, groups). Today it can only be edited in the setup file | Stage 3a-1 | deferred |
| Revisit who may reach each app if the platform ever runs on a shared server rather than a laptop (the setup app is already limited to this computer; the dashboard is open to the network) | Stage 1e | deferred until a shared server is planned |

## Proof of concept (on `main`)
| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| Python with Streamlit | what you asked for; runs on this laptop, offline |
| Five parts kept separate: settings, virtual lab, cell simulator, decision engine, dashboard | so each can be changed or replaced without breaking the others; enforced by an automated import check |
| The dashboard only displays; its one calculation is trials per week divided by the manual baseline | keeps the dashboard trustworthy and simple; the exception is documented and guarded |
| The decision engine never sees the lab's hidden truth | otherwise results would flatter the engine; scoring after the fact uses a separate module |
| Settings in one YAML file with plain-English error messages | you can change numbers without reading code |
| One trial at a time; overlapping trials left as a future extension | matches the 4-hour cycle and keeps the first simulator simple |
| About 3% of random recipes truly good | a fixed list should not find a pass almost at once |
| Headline measure: first trial whose recipe is truly good (scored after the fact) | noise cannot flatter it; the measured-pass measure is shown second |
| 40 paired campaigns of 30 trials; seeds fixed in advance (2026 to 2065); no tuning on them | fair comparison; no choosing seeds after seeing results |
| Comparison without faults as the headline, with faults available | isolates the effect of learning |
| Adaptive engine = Gaussian-process model choosing the recipe most likely to pass both targets (a simple Bayesian optimisation) | learns from every result, says how sure it is, explains each choice |
| Scripted faults at the dispense step: trial 8 recovers, trial 19 fails its retry | shows recovery, skipping and an alert for the next working day |
| Cleaning follows a skipped candidate (a setting, on by default) | a clog is when you would clean |
| Trial counts written "about 29 to 30", never a bare number | the count hangs on a 30-minute margin |
| Run first, then replay; the dashboard reads one run-record file | simple, offline, and the dashboard stays logic-free |
| What the page shows must not reveal what is not yet known | a bug showed a result early; a check now guards it |
| Charts bundle their library; Open Sans font bundled | must work in a demo room with no internet |
| Deloitte brand styling, colours only from the palette (checked) | your request; avoids off-brand colours |
| `main` stays the safe PoC; new work on `platform-build` | the working demo cannot be damaged |
| `CLAUDE.md` holds the standing working rules | rules persist across sessions |

## Platform build (on `platform-build`)
| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| Reference viewer and mock data live in `reference/`, `data/mock/`, `tools/` | keep them apart from the running code |
| Mock data is synthetic and never used to tune models; test on freshly generated seeds | tuning to it would make models look better than they are |
| Setup screens are a **separate small Streamlit app**, with one launcher (`run.bat`) offering both apps | setup must validate input, the dashboard must calculate nothing; a client still sees one product |
| Shared validation module with plain-English messages | the screens and health checks use the same rules |
| Use only scikit-learn, scipy, statsmodels (plus openpyxl and reportlab) **(as built: only scikit-learn was added in Stage 3a, scipy came with it, and statsmodels stays out; see "Stage 3a")** | wheels exist for Python 3.14 (dry run); deep learning is unnecessary and heavy |
| The "18%" is the share of historical rows; random recipes pass 6.6% to 11.2% depending on setting and noise | historical rows cluster near good recipes; the measured figures are in `docs/platform_plan.md` |
| "Hard" mock data means harder to model, not rarer to pass | hard has more interactions, noise, drift and gaps; its pass share is slightly higher |
| Validation holds out whole batches, whole suppliers and later dates, never random rows | random rows leak replicates and batch effects and flatter the model |
| A sealed, structurally different second test lab, used only for final acceptance **(status: CREATED AND SEALED on 2026-10-11, after Stage 3 rather than before it as planned; fingerprints in "The sealed acceptance lab" at the end of this file)** | catches models that only work on the generator they were built against |
| Stage 3 split into 3a (preparation, candidate models, grouped validation) and 3b (calibration, applicability warning, scorecard, decision-usefulness replay) | each part can be checked on its own |
| Plan order: setup and validation, ingestion, modelling (3a, 3b), engine, configurable simulator, executor and motion plan, 3D viewer, dashboard integration, reports, hardening | each stage rests on the one before |
| The motion plan is data; a Python stand-in executor now, Gazebo or hardware later | the same plan can later run on other executors |
| The 3D viewer only replays a plan (no logic) and bundles its library and fonts | keeps the "no logic" rule and works offline |
| Viewer screenshot check uses headless **Chrome** with software rendering; Edge headless is blocked by company policy; if it fails, a by-eye checklist is used | tested on this machine: the reference viewer's 3D scene rendered |
| The PoC and its five checks stay unchanged and are re-run at every stage | "never change existing behaviour without asking" |

## Format review: the fourteen decisions, and the answers to the review points
| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| Ten action names: move, pick, place, pour, shake, press, stir, scan, read, wait (`read_instrument` becomes `read`; `tap_shake` becomes `shake` with style "tap"; no `event` in plans) | a small fixed vocabulary is easy to validate, plan and animate |
| Two files: the **motion plan** is intent only; the **executed timeline** is what the executor returns (same actions plus actual times, outcomes, faults, recovery actions tagged `planned` or `recovery`); the viewer replays the executed timeline | a plan can be written before anything happens; faults only exist after execution |
| Every action has a `step`; the plan header has `trial_start_minute`; within each step the action durations, waits included, add up to the setup's step time within 1 second | ties every action to the setup's timings and to the run clock |
| One ID per quantity with the unit in the name (`dissolution_30min_pct`, `hardness_kp`, `friability_pct`, ...); lowercase phases; rules in `docs/naming.md`; raw columns map to these in ingestion and PoC names map through the converter | removes the hardness / hardness_kp style confusion |
| `data_status` values: synthetic, illustrative, real, validated; a record takes its weakest input | one honest label that can only get stronger when every input does |
| `batch_size_g` in the setup (illustrative 20 g) | pour amounts need a batch size |
| Targets split into constraints (at least, at most, between) and objectives (maximise, minimise), each with a unit | "must be met" and "push as far as possible" are different |
| Faults get a structured `where` (step, action, ingredient); `chance_per_trial` and `retry_succeeds_chance` are probabilities used only for random faults | the planner must know where a fault belongs |
| A `data:` section in the setup (column mapping, newtons divided by 9.807, below-detection rule, replicates, missing values, failed runs); ingestion tolerates a byte-order mark and Windows line endings | how to read the client's table must live with the setup |
| Layout gets an object list, tool positions, shoulder height and link lengths; reach is checked in 3D | the 3D viewer and the executor need the arm's geometry |
| Machine-readable JSON Schemas for all formats; YAML read with the safe reader only; file references relative, inside the project, no `..` | catches typing mistakes and unsafe files |
| CSV without byte-order mark and with the mock's line endings; companion `.meta.json` with version and synthetic label; a second, messy example from the hard data | a CSV cannot carry its own label; the messy example exercises ingestion |
| Run record: every section present or marked `unchanged_from_poc`; model numbers null with a note | no realistic-looking numbers where no model exists |
| `check_formats.bat`: a permanent script that checks all of the above in plain words | the formats stay correct as the platform grows |

| Review point | Answer |
|---|---|
| Order of `data_status` | synthetic is weakest, then illustrative, real, validated (taken from the order given; approved by committing) |
| Does the example plan use the friability tester? | Yes (3 actions plus the 5-minute tumble). The setup needs it because friability is an objective, so it stays. **Rule: the planner includes friability steps only when the setup needs them**; a check enforces it |
| Fault timing | platform fault timing comes from the executor and will not match the PoC's rule; `main` is unchanged |
| Reach margin | warn below 0.10 m of spare reach, fail below 0.03 m; the example is at 0.080 m (a warning, layout unchanged) |
| Filler waits | every wait has a `reason` naming the real process it stands for (for example "friability drum tumbling the tablet for a fixed number of rotations"); a check enforces it |
| Example files | hand-authored until Stage 6, when the real planner generates plans that must pass the same checks |
| Trial-8 story | `trial8_story.md` is generated by `tools/make_trial_story.py` from the executed timeline and checked against it |
| BOM and CRLF tolerance | a Stage 2 requirement, proven now in the format check's reader |

## Stage 1 (setup app, validation, launcher): what was built, and what changed from the plan
Built as planned: ten setup screens, one shared checker with plain-English messages, saving and loading of setups, a converter to the
proof of concept's settings, and a launcher menu. The five proof-of-concept checks and the dashboard are unchanged.

| What changed or was added | Why |
|---|---|
| Schemas and the four example files the setup app reads moved into the package (`cellpoc/formats/`); `docs/platform_formats/` keeps identical copies and a pointer. **Four files moved, not two**: the experiments table and its companion file travel with the example setup so it can pass the "file exists" rule. `check_formats` verifies each docs copy equals its package master | programs must not read from `docs/`; the bundled example has to validate on its own |
| Setups carry a `format_version`. Older versions load (through registered upgrades); a newer version than the program knows is refused with a plain message. The schema accepts any whole number and the program decides | a saved setup must stay readable as the format grows, and must never be silently misread |
| Saved setups record **when** (UTC), **which program version** and a **SHA-256 fingerprint of the content**, and no personal names. Saving is refused while any error remains and writes only into the git-ignored `setups/` folder | a saved file can be traced and checked for later edits without identifying anyone |
| 99 messages (rules R01 to R54 plus file, version and generic ones), each with a test; `docs/setup_messages.md` is generated from the catalogue and the tests; the check fails if any message has no test or the document is out of date | wording is reviewable without reading code, and nothing ships untested |
| Choosing data status "real" or "validated" warns that the client's data-handling policy must be approved before real data is used (rule R04) | a reminder at the moment it matters |
| Unit rules (R12, R19): an error only when the units clearly conflict; unknown units, a missing unit ending or a capital-letter difference are warnings. The allowed units live in one list (`cellpoc/setup/units.py`) | avoids false errors, and one edit changes everything |
| 200 random breakages of the example setup must never crash the checker or show technical wording (schema, null, traceback ...). Quoted text the person typed is not judged | proves the checker copes with whatever a person might write |
| The converter to PoC settings is a **preview only**: it builds text, cannot write any file, and `config/settings.yaml` is checked byte for byte. It refuses (with a plain note) what the proof of concept cannot represent, and its output is checked against the PoC's own settings checker across 306 setups | the PoC is smaller than a setup (five named ingredients, two targets, no process settings) |
| The setup app is tested with Streamlit's test harness (28 checks, including 25 damaged setups drawn on all ten screens). The harness cannot judge appearance, so `docs/setup_app.md` carries a by-eye checklist | stated honestly instead of implied |
| **Correction to the plan:** Streamlit pages *do* render in headless Chrome screenshots, if the browser is given a long virtual wait (`--virtual-time-budget=600000`; 60 seconds gave blank or half-drawn pictures). `tools/take_setup_screenshots.py` takes the ten screens in two states; pictures go to `docs/screenshots/` (not committed) | the earlier finding was a timing problem, not a limit |
| `checks/architecture.py` lists the new parts (`formats`, `setup`, `setup_app`) and fails if the setup parts import a network library. Each of the five PoC checks therefore gained one line; none was weakened or removed. The reach calculation is shared between `check_formats` and the setup checker (same result, 71 checks) | keeps the parts separate as the platform grows |
| A saved setup also finds the bundled example layout and table (a third place to look, limited to the bundled folder) | a setup saved into `setups/` must reopen |
| `run.bat` is a menu (1 dashboard on port 8501, 2 setup app on port 8502, 3 both, each in its own window); `run.bat dashboard` starts the dashboard as before and `run.bat setup` the setup app. A busy port gets a plain-words message. The interim `run_setup_app.bat` was removed | one launcher, as planned |
| The setup app is started with `--server.address localhost`, so no other computer on the network can open it. **The dashboard was left as it was** (Streamlit listens on the network by default). Decision: restricting the setup app is approved; the dashboard stays open to the network for now. **Revisit this if the platform ever runs on a shared server rather than a laptop** (then both apps need a deliberate access decision: who may reach them, and over what connection) | the setup app will handle client files, so it is kept on this computer only |
| `check_all.bat` runs the five PoC checks, the format check and the platform check and prints one table with the total time | one double-click for "is everything still fine" |
| `requirements.lock.txt` holds the exact versions of all 39 packages; `requirements.txt` stays the readable list | an environment can be rebuilt exactly (it was rebuilt this way after the project moved folders) |
| Public real datasets for testing ingestion and models live in `data/public/` (git-ignored data, committed `SOURCES.md` with DOIs, licences and citations). They are never presented as client data; the batch-production set is CC BY 4.0 so attribution is kept | real third-party data for tests, with the licence terms recorded |
| Setup files and client data stay out of git (`setups/`, `data/client/`); data and format files are pinned to Unix line endings (`.gitattributes`) | no client data in the repository; stable fingerprints |

## Stage 2 (data ingestion): what was delivered (2a to 2e) and the decisions behind it
**Delivered:** optional settings in the setup for reading files (2a); a safe reader for CSV, text and Excel `.xlsx` (2b); typing, units, missing and
below-detection values, dates and the weight check (2c); failed runs, replicates and joins with exact row accounting (2d); and the quality report with its health check (2e).
`check_ingest.bat` runs 106 checks; `check_all.bat` runs 479 in all. Tested on the three synthetic mock files, freshly generated mock files, and the two real public datasets (four files).
Nothing in the proof of concept, the dashboard or the earlier stages was changed.

| Decision (2e) | Why |
|---|---|
| The quality report is one Markdown document, written by `cellpoc/ingest/report.py` (`tools/make_quality_report.py`, double-click `make_example_reports.bat`; reports go to `runs/ingest/`, not git). Its **title carries the data-status label** and **the very next thing is the row-accounting block** (`RowAccount.headline()` word for word, in a code block so the capitals survive), then the data status of every file, what needs attention, and every assumption written out | dropped rows, failed runs and the data's status can never hide further down |
| **Data status:** the label for each file comes from the table's companion file (the mocks: SYNTHETIC) or from a new optional `data.provenance` setting in the setup (the public data: "REAL PUBLIC DATA, NOT CLIENT DATA"); the report takes the weakest status of all files (the Stage 1 rule); real data that might be client data carries the data-handling reminder; no stated status is labelled ILLUSTRATIVE | every output is labelled until real client data and expert review exist |
| **Assumptions are stated, not logged:** the report has a section that lists each assumed unit (with the dataset's reason), the kg/cm2-to-kP conversion, the mg-to-percent weight check (what it divides by, the tolerance, how many rows are beyond it, that nothing is rescaled), an assumed date format, month-only dates, and every use of the 2-digit-year rule; the column table marks every assumed unit **ASSUMED** and a check confirms that exactly the three expected columns carry it | an assumption must never look like a fact |
| The report repeats every warning under "What needs your attention" and lists every message the reader produced; it contains no clock time (the same input always gives the same report) and may quote a few example values from the file | repeatable and complete |
| A file that cannot be used still gets a labelled report that opens with "no table was produced" and lists the errors | a failure is information too |
| **Stage 2 as a whole** (decisions already recorded above, in the sections for 2a to 2d): the plan approved with a second stop after 2c; Excel and pandas approved; 2% as the weight tolerance with the exact gap kept per row; only the one unit the dictionary truly does not list is marked assumed; the 2-digit-year rule visible in the docs and every report; dropped rows a warning; the Laboratory/Normalization batch-size discrepancy (codes 14 and 16) recorded as a data-quality issue in the public data, with the anchored check left as its permanent record | the sequence of approvals |

### Stage 2 decisions by step
| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| Plan approved, with a **second stop after 2c** (typing, units, missing and below-detection values, dates) before 2d (replicates, failed runs, joins) | 2c holds the real judgment calls |
| Libraries approved: `openpyxl==3.1.5` and `et_xmlfile==2.0.0` (Excel .xlsx), and the already-installed `pandas==3.0.6` used directly; all three are in `requirements.txt` and the lock file (41 packages) | the plan needs Excel files; pandas was already installed through Streamlit |
| The setup's `data:` section gained **optional** settings: `delimiter`, `decimal`, `sheet`, `header_row`, `missing_markers`, `date` (format, language, month names), `column_units`, `joins`; the file name and the column mapping are the only required parts; an empty or missing id column means "this file has no such column". The format version stays 1 | only optional additions, so older setups still load; real files (a literature table with no ids, batch tables with no failed-run column) need parts to be optional |
| 18 new setup messages (117 rule tests); the checker and the reader share one way of looking at a file's columns (`cellpoc/setup/tablefile.py`): byte-order mark, UTF-16, declared encoding, separator detection, names compared ignoring capital letters and extra spaces | a semicolon file must not be reported as having one column |
| The reader lives in `cellpoc/ingest/` (imports only the setup part; no network). It reads CSV, text and `.xlsx`; reports every repair; never fills in or guesses silently; row accounting is exact (rows read = rows kept + blank rows skipped) | trustworthy numbers for the quality report |
| Refused: Excel files with macros (.xlsm), binary (.xlsb) and old (.xls); files over 100 MB; Excel files that unpack to over 500 MB; tables over 2 million rows or 5,000 columns; a `.csv` that is really Excel; binary data. Formulas are never evaluated | safety, and clear messages instead of surprises |
| A file that is not valid in its declared encoding is an **error** (not silently re-read); with no declared encoding, invalid UTF-8 falls back to Windows-1252 with a warning that points to windows-1250 | no silent misreading of names such as č, š, ž |
| A random-damage test found a latent Stage 1 crash (an unhashable `hardness.to` value); fixed | exactly what that test is for |
| Setup app: new settings can be edited in the YAML now; Data-screen fields for them are not added yet (optional later step) | scope kept to what was approved |
| **2c, missing values:** only an empty cell and the markers the setup lists (`missing_markers`, compared ignoring capitals) mean "no value"; every marker found is counted in a message. `#N/A` is **not** assumed: unlisted, it is reported as "not a number" with a hint. Nothing is ever filled in | no silent assumption about what a cell means |
| **2c, numbers:** a cell that is not clearly a number (`12.5`, `12,5`, `1e-3`, `+5`, `.5`) becomes missing and is reported; if more than half of a column's values are not numbers it is an error (the column is probably mapped wrongly). `1,234.5`, `inf`, `nan` and similar are refused. Values that look impossible (a percentage outside 0 to 100, a negative amount) are **kept and warned about**, never changed | real data has surprises (drug content above 100% of label claim is genuine); changing them would hide them |
| **2c, below-detection:** `<0.05` in a note column or inside a value follows the setup's rule (use the limit, half the limit, or missing) and is flagged in `<id>__below_detection`; with no rule in the setup, such a value is treated as missing and reported. `>100` values are treated as missing and reported | the rule is a modelling choice that belongs to the setup, not to the reader |
| **2c, dates:** read exactly as the setup's `date.format` says (`yyyy-mm-dd`, `dd.mm.yyyy`, `mon.yy` ...); month names come from built-in English and Slovenian tables (or twelve names in the setup), never from the computer's language settings; day and month are never guessed (with no format, only year-month-day is read and a warning says so); a 2-digit year 00 to 69 is 2000 to 2069 and 70 to 99 is 1970 to 1999; a format with no day gives the first of the month and is marked month-only, with a warning that the order of events inside a month is unknown (it matters for "validate on later dates"); real Excel dates are always read exactly | `Laboratory.csv` has Slovenian `nov.18` dates; guessing day and month silently is how data gets corrupted |
| **2c, units:** only conversions the setup declares are applied (hardness: newtons divided by 9.807 to kP; kg/cm2 divided by 1 **marked assumed**); a unit with no conversion leaves the value missing and is reported; a column whose declared unit differs from the setup's, with no conversion, is used as written with a warning. A column or conversion listed under `assumed_units` / marked `status: assumed` is reported as assumed everywhere it appears (message, column report, and the percentage made from it) | an assumption must never look like a fact |
| **2c, mg to percent (literature table):** amounts (IDs ending `_mg`) are divided by the total weight column (`Weight(mg)`, confirmed by the dataset's own dictionary as "the total weight of tablet, mg"), so missing material stays visible instead of being rescaled to 100%. The exact signed gap, (sum of amounts - weight) / weight in percent, is kept for every row in `mass_balance_gap_pct` (negative = the listed amounts fall short) with a flag `mass_balance_flag` when the gap is **more than the stated tolerance, 2%**. A gap of exactly 2.0% passes. A missing or zero weight, or a missing amount, leaves that row's gap empty and is reported. Result on the real file: 118 of 1,982 rows (6.0%) flagged; all fall short, none overshoots, the worst is 32% short | approved: 2% tolerance, exact gap attached to every flag |
| **2c, dictionary check:** of the 56 excipient columns in the literature file, the dataset's own dictionary lists 55 once capitals and spaces are ignored (`Sodium saccharin` and `dibasic calcium phosphate` differ from their dictionary entries only by capital letters); only `Sodium Saccharine` is not listed (the dictionary has `Sodium Saccharin`). The fixture therefore marks that one column "unit assumed, not confirmed by the dataset's dictionary" and treats it as mg; the check derives the list from the dictionary independently | the label must be true for each column it is on |
| **2c, correction:** an earlier summary said 195 rows (9.8%) fail the 2% tolerance. That count was wrong: it tested `abs(ratio - 1) <= 0.02` in floating point, so rows exactly 2% short (77 of them) failed by about 1e-16. The correct count is 118. The same slip had inflated the counts at 1% (241 not 304) and 0.5% (401 not 477) | the reader and the independent recount both round before comparing |
| **2c, fixtures:** `cellpoc/ingest/fixtures/` holds three small setup files describing how to read `Laboratory.csv`, `Process.csv` and the literature table (generated once from the dataset's own header and dictionary; no data). They are ingestion fixtures, not full setups. The mock files are read with the bundled example setup | test the reader on real files without committing any of them |
| **2d, two-digit years are never invisible:** the rule (00 to 69 mean 2000 to 2069; 70 to 99 mean 1970 to 1999) is one named constant in the code, stated in its own section of `docs/ingest_messages.md`, and every date read that way raises a warning (I71, with the count and an example) and a note in the date column's report. Four-digit years raise nothing | approved addition |
| **2d, dropped rows cannot hide:** rows dropped for missing values are a **warning** (I58, "ROWS DROPPED"), not a note; a row-accounting message (I84) is a warning whenever anything was removed; and `RowAccount.headline()` is the text block the quality report (step 2e) must open with: dropped rows and failed runs in capitals with their counts, then the counts adding up. The report itself is built in 2e, where a check will confirm the block is first and prominent | approved addition; the report does not exist yet, so only its source of truth is built and tested now |
| **2d, order of row handling:** failed runs first (their gaps are expected and must not be counted as missing values), then the missing-value policy, then replicates. Rows read = rows dropped for missing values + failed runs set aside + replicate rows combined + rows left, always (tested on 120 random tables) | every row is accounted for |
| **2d, failed runs:** matched ignoring capitals and spaces; policy `keep` leaves them in the table flagged `is_failed`; `exclude` removes them and keeps nothing; `exclude_from_models_keep_in_failure_statistics` removes them from the model table but keeps them in a separate table with statistics by reason, batch and month. The values found in the status column are always listed (I76) so an unexpected status is visible | a failure is information |
| **2d, replicates:** rows with the same value in the setup's `group_by` columns (output names such as `experiment_id`, or the file's own column names) are combined by the rule: `mean` or `median` of the values that are present (a group with no value stays empty), `any` for flags such as below-detection, the earliest date, the first value of a text column (with a warning when replicates disagree). The replicate-number column is dropped, `replicate_count` is added, and the individual rows are kept for noise estimates together with the pooled standard deviation of each response and its degrees of freedom. `keep_all` keeps every row. A row with no ID is never merged with another (an unknown is not a match) | replicate scatter is the raw material for noise estimates |
| **2d, joins:** a left join on key columns (compared ignoring capitals and spaces around); every main row is kept; rows with no match get empty cells and are counted (I79); a joined file with two rows for one key is refused (I78); joined columns can take a prefix, and a name clash is refused (I83). Joined files are read with automatic separator and encoding detection | `Laboratory.csv` + `Process.csv` + `Normalization.csv` must work together |
| **2d, a real inconsistency found in the public data (not changed, not hidden):** 13 `Laboratory.csv` rows (11 of product code 14, 2 of product code 16) give the batch size as 4,800,000, while `Normalization.csv` lists 480,000 for both codes (ten times less). Every other code agrees, and the normalisation factor is always the Normalization batch size divided by 100,000. One of the two files has a slip; which one cannot be told from the data. The check is anchored to exactly this known discrepancy | an honest note about the test data |
| **2c, missing-value policy in code:** `skip_for_that_response` keeps every row; `drop_row` drops a row when any value the setup requires (ingredients, process settings, context factors, required targets; or every mapped column when the setup lists none) is missing, and lists each dropped row; `fail` refuses the table | rows are only ever removed on purpose, and listed |

## Stage 3a (modelling): decisions, in the order they were made
Approved plan, with four changes (below). Built in steps: 3a-1 (libraries and the modelling table), 3a-2 (grouped splitters and guards, **stop for review**), 3a-3 (models, **stop** to review fast-mode results on the built models), 3a-4 (the full comparison, report, docs; planned as a multi-hour run, **measured at 9 to 16 minutes**, see "Stage 3a-4"). **Status: DONE, tagged `stage3a-complete`.** This section is a record in the order decisions were made: where a later entry replaces an earlier one it says so.

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| **Library:** only `scikit-learn==1.9.1` added (it brings scipy, joblib, threadpoolctl, cloudpickle; the lock file goes from 41 to 46 packages; nothing already pinned changed). PyTorch, LightGBM, XGBoost, MAPIE and statsmodels stay out | all five were tested and worked, none is needed; scikit-learn covers every model, and conformal intervals take a few lines by hand |
| **Gaussian process made feasible by two stages, not by a sparse variant:** the search for the settings (the slow part) runs on a fixed-seed sample of about 500 training rows taken from training groups only; the exact GP is then computed on every training row. The exact fit is skipped above 5,000 training rows and the report says so (that limit is extrapolated from a 2,300-row measurement, not measured). Measured on made-up data, 2,300 training rows: error 0.013 to 0.014 against 0.012 for the full classic fit (262 s) and 0.018 for the classic fit capped at 1,000 rows | nearly the full accuracy at a fraction of the time, with no new library |
| **Literature table (`final Data All Exipients.csv`):** no API name, paper ID or date exists. "Grouped" means leave whole APIs out, with the API inferred from identical molecular descriptors (126 groups, matching the dataset's own list of 126 APIs). Rows from one paper that studies several APIs can still share conditions. Its results go in a separate section headed "robustness test only" and are **excluded from every learning-accuracy claim**, including the Stage 3b scorecard | it cannot test new batches, suppliers, dates or papers |
| **Batch dataset (`Laboratory.csv` + `Process.csv`):** batch = `api_batch` (238 API lots; the file's own `batch` column has one row per batch, so it cannot be held out); supplier = `api_code` (5 values), **labelled "proxy, not a confirmed supplier"** because the file has no supplier column and does not say what the code stands for; time = `start` (month precision, 29 months; every batch in a month shares one time value). Product code and strength go in as categories | the closest honest stand-ins the file offers |
| **Proxy wording:** wherever `api_code` is used as the supplier split (every report heading, table, chart and note, not just the methodology), it carries the exact phrase "proxy, not a confirmed supplier". The phrase is written in one place in the code (`PROXY_PHRASE`) and a check fails if it is written anywhere else or missing from a heading | requested change 3 |
| **Leakage:** the setup's new `data.validation.derived_from` names columns calculated from others; a calculated column and the columns it comes from are never used to predict each other, in either direction. Checked on the real data: tensile strength matches 2 x force / (pi x diameter x thickness) (about 99.9% of its variation is explained by hardness, diameter and thickness, for tablet cores and film-coated tablets), and `Process.csv` carries six exact copies of Laboratory results (drug release average and minimum, residual solvent, total impurities, impurity O, impurity L; identical in all 987 batches that have values). Every test result of the finished tablet is a result, never an input. **Not verified:** the source paper's own list of derived columns is not in the files we hold (the "Code availability" document only describes the process-sensor features), so the list comes from the standard formula and the tests above and should be checked against the paper | a model must not be handed the answer |
| **Automatic safety net:** an input whose correlation with the result (Pearson or Spearman) is 0.999 or more is treated as a copy and left out, with a message. (The first draft used 0.99; a very strong honest input scored 0.990 in a test, so the line moved to 0.999. Exact copies score 1.0000.) **Stated limit:** a product formula such as tensile strength is not caught by correlation, which is why it must be declared. A later check (3a-3) will flag any held-out score above 0.99 R-squared as "check for leakage" | catches copies without blocking a strong honest predictor |
| **Compositional ingredients:** the ingredient percentages add up to 100, so the balance ingredient (the setup's `role: balance`, the filler in the example) is left out of the inputs; the other percentages are used as they are. In the literature table the "unlisted remainder" (100 minus the listed percentages) is implied by the listed columns, so it is not added as an input. Checked: the mock percentages add up to 100 in every row; the literature's listed percentages never exceed the tablet weight | an unconstrained model would see impossible mixtures and a perfectly redundant column |
| **Flagged literature rows (the 118 whose listed amounts fall short of the tablet weight by more than 2%): kept, with the flag carried through.** They stay in training and in testing for every result; the table carries `mass_balance_flag`; the reports show scores with and without them. They are not removed and not repaired | they fall short by 2.05% to 32% (most likely an unlisted ingredient), sit in blocks of consecutive rows (likely whole articles, so removing them would drop whole papers and bias the sample), and the shortfall is visible to the models in the listed percentages; the with-and-without comparison tells us whether they matter |
| **Assumed hardness unit (literature table):** the assumption covers the whole column (all 1,982 rows), not a subset, so there are no rows to include or exclude. The conversion divides by 1, so no value changes; only the label kP is assumed. Hardness stays a modelled result and is written "hardness_kp (unit assumed)" in every table, heading and report. The one assumed ingredient unit (`Sodium Saccharine`) is carried the same way | an assumption must never look like a fact |
| **Zero-heavy literature columns left out of modelling** (drug content, water absorption ratio, wetting time: 755, 1,046 and 462 zeros), confirmed by the project owner, since nobody on the team can confirm the zeros are real measurements. Listed in every report as a limitation for a future formulation expert to review. Modelled results from this table: hardness, friability, disintegration time | requested confirmation |
| **Baselines in every table:** predict-the-training-mean and nearest-neighbour lookup, scored on identical folds beside every candidate; quadratic regression stays a candidate | a model is only as good as its margin over a naive answer |
| **Calibration is measured separately from conformal prediction:** for every uncertainty method (GP, forest spread, ensemble spread, conformal) the report gives how often "90%" intervals contain the truth on held-out groups, plus the interval width. Conformal is one candidate; its calibration set is whole groups kept apart from the fitting set. 3a reports and flags; judging and fixing belong to 3b | a method can claim 90% and deliver 70% |
| **Joint versus separate fitting is stated in every report and table from 3a-3 on** (requested change 2): which model families are fit jointly across the results and which are fit independently per result, as a stated limitation | not left implicit |
| **Selection rule (carried from the plan, unchanged):** a model's score is the worst of the hold-out types, normalised by the result's spread; it must beat the baseline in every available type, otherwise the report says "no model beats the baseline"; ties within 5% go to the simpler or faster model; the report shows everything either way. Health-check self-tests carried: altering test labels never changes training-side results; a result hiding among the inputs is blocked; noise-free GP interpolation is recovered; GP uncertainty grows away from the training data; conformal coverage is near 90%; injected random splits are caught | the plan as approved |
| **Extra stop after 3a-3** (requested change 4): the fast-mode results on the built models are shown before the full multi-hour comparison starts | you see real results on the real models first |
| **Estimated runtime of the full comparison:** about 3 hours (plausible range 1.5 to 4.5), sequential on this laptop, dominated by the Gaussian process; saved after every fold so it can be stopped and resumed; not part of `check_all`. The health check uses a fast mode of about 2 minutes. An estimate, to be replaced by a measurement **(replaced: the full run took 15 min 37 s and then 9 min 16 s, see 3a-3 and 3a-4 below)** | stated before building, as asked |
| **New setup section `data.validation` (optional; format version stays 1):** `responses`, `inputs` (left out means the setup's ingredients without the balance, process settings and context factors), `derived_from`, `left_out` (column and reason), `groups` (batch, supplier, date, api; a supplier group must say `confirmed: true` or `false`). Nine new setup messages (R69 in five wordings, then R70 to R73), each with a test (134 setup tests in all). Not yet on the setup app's Data screen: added to the deferred-polish list | the client must say what counts as a batch, a supplier and a date |
| **Modelling table (`cellpoc/modelling/`):** for every result it records which rows and which inputs are used; rows with a missing result or input are left out for that result, counted and shown, never filled in; all models in a comparison use the same rows; context factors (product code, strength) are categories whatever their values look like. 21 modelling messages, each with a test (`docs/modelling_messages.md`). The part imports only `setup` and `ingest`, uses no network and never sees the hidden truth (enforced by the architecture check) | one place decides, and records, what the models see |
| **Found while building, left for you to decide:** in the real batch data the input `api_l_impurity` is empty in 370 of 1,005 batches, so requiring it leaves 633 batches for every result (`api_total_impurities` costs 67 more). The modelling table reports this; the table does not drop the column on its own | dropping the column would keep more rows but lose an input; that is your call |

### Stage 3a-2: the grouped splitters
| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| **One place divides rows** (`cellpoc/modelling/splits.py`), and it never uses random rows. Three hold-out types: **whole batches** (or whole APIs for the literature table) in 5 folds of whole groups; **whole suppliers** held out one at a time when there are 10 or fewer (otherwise 5 folds of whole groups); **later dates** in 4 forward steps (the earliest half of the distinct dates is always training; the later half is tested in 4 consecutive blocks, each trained on everything earlier). Groups are dealt into folds biggest first, ties by name, so the same table always gives the same folds whatever order its rows are stored in | random rows would put replicates and batch effects on both sides of the split and flatter every model |
| **A date is never split:** a forward step cuts between distinct dates (for month-only data, between months), so every test date is strictly later than every training date. In the mock files each batch sits on one date, so a batch is never split either. Where a table's batch column spans many dates (the API lots in the real batch data), dates are cut by date only, not by batch | the batch data's lots are used across many months; aligning to them would remove most rows |
| **Fixed in advance, never changed after seeing a score:** at least 10 test rows and 30 training rows per fold (smaller folds are dropped with a message), at least 6 distinct dates for forward steps, 5 folds, 4 steps, leave-one-supplier-out up to 10 suppliers | no tuning of the validation to flatter a model |
| **A hold-out that cannot be run is skipped with a plain message and never replaced by random rows.** Consequences on the test tables: the **small mock has no forward steps** (only 4 dates); the **literature table runs only leave-APIs-out** (it has no batch, supplier or date); rows with no group value are left out of that hold-out, counted and said | honest about what each table can and cannot test |
| **Guards:** every plan is checked before it is handed out by a route independent of the one that built it (no row in both training and test; no group on both sides; no row tested twice; every test date later than every training date) and a leak raises an error before any model is fitted. The check plants a random split, a backwards-in-time split, duplicated rows and a twice-tested row and confirms each is caught, runs 200 random tables, and scans the modelling code for any random splitter (`train_test_split`, `KFold`, `ShuffleSplit`, cross-validation and search helpers, sampling, shuffling, and a model's own hidden validation split such as `early_stopping=True`) | "never random rows" is enforced, not promised |
| **The method sentence says "groups", and the heading names them:** for the supplier stand-in the heading and every line that names `api_code` carry "proxy, not a confirmed supplier" (checked line by line in the fold map) | requested change 3 |
| **Fold maps:** `make_fold_maps.bat` (or `tools/make_fold_maps.py`) writes one plain document per table into `runs/modelling/` (not in git): the data-status label first, the statement that no random rows are used, then each hold-out with every fold (what is held out, training rows, test rows) and whatever was skipped and why | something you can read without code |
| **Seen in the fold maps:** because the real batch data's result rows are limited to batches with every input present (633 of 1,005), only 147 of the 238 API lots remain in the batch hold-out and the last forward step tests only 27 batches. This is the missing-`api_l_impurity` effect already recorded above | the splitters show the cost of the missing-input rule |

### Stage 3a, the batch data: two comparisons, kept apart (decided 2026-10-11)
Requiring `api_l_impurity` (empty in 370 of 1,005 batches) and `api_total_impurities` (empty in 67) for every batch was costing most of the table. The decision: build both, clearly separated.

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| **Primary comparison (`batch_production`; used for the Stage 3b scorecard and any accuracy claim):** the two impurity columns are not required inputs; they are listed as left out, with the reason. This keeps **977 of 1,005 batches** (97%; the other 28 lack some other input), **231 of 238 API lots**, all **29 of 29 months**, and large folds throughout (forward steps test 108, 115, 75 and 120 batches; the smallest supplier fold tests 15) | the headline comparison should be on nearly all the data, with every hold-out viable |
| **Extended comparison (`batch_production_extended`; exploratory only, never in the headline scorecard):** both columns are required, which restricts every result to **633 batches**. Every heading of every report about it carries: "EXPLORATORY: exploratory, smaller and non-random sample (only batches where both impurity measurements exist), missingness tied to one API source (every missing value is in the batches of a single API code), not part of the main comparison". No model is chosen from it | it asks a different question (do the impurity measurements help?) on a sample that is not representative |
| **Measured before building, as asked: do whole API lots disappear in the extended comparison?** **Yes, entirely, not just thinned.** 91 of the 238 API lots (38%) have no batch left: all 323 of their batches have an empty `api_l_impurity`, and every one of those lots belongs to API code 3. Of the lots that remain, 12 keep only some of their batches. The whole-batch (lot) folds still work (5 folds of 126 to 127 batches), but they cover only the 147 lots that survive. The supplier stand-in is hit hardest: API code 3 falls from 481 batches to 109 in its held-out fold. The forward steps keep 28 of 29 months but the last step tests only 27 batches | exactly the thinning of the date folds, only worse for the lots |
| **In the primary comparison, 7 lots disappear entirely** (26 batches, all in API code 3, from the 28 batches that lack some other input); 231 of 238 lots remain | stated so the small loss is visible |
| **The missingness is concentrated, which the data confirms:** every missing `api_l_impurity` value (370) is in API code 3 (370 of its 481 batches), and every missing `api_total_impurities` value (67) is too. 0 of the 150, 15, 24 and 335 batches in the other four codes are missing either. **Wording note:** the requested label said "per the source paper". I had not seen what the paper says, so the first label stated only what the data shows. **Update 2026-10-11: the project owner confirmed from the source paper's "Missing data" section that the missingness is attributed to a single API material source, and the label now says so ("confirmed in the source paper's Missing data section")** | the label must be true as written |
| **Mechanism:** a new optional setup setting `data.validation.exploratory` (a plain sentence). When present, every heading of every report and fold map carries it and no model is chosen. A table with only a leave-APIs-out hold-out (the literature table) carries "ROBUSTNESS TEST ONLY: not a claim about learning accuracy" in the same way | one mechanism for both kinds of table that must not support a headline claim |

### Stage 3a-3: the models, the selection rule and the report (fast mode)
| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| **Two naive baselines and six candidates, one small interface** (`cellpoc/modelling/models.py`): predict-the-training-mean; nearest-neighbour lookup; quadratic regression (ridge, penalty 1); gradient-boosted trees with split-conformal ranges; random forest; a 5-member neural-network ensemble; the Gaussian process (two-stage: settings searched on a fixed-seed sample of 500 training rows, then the exact model on every training row; one length scale per input up to 30 inputs, one shared length scale above that; skipped above 5,000 training rows); and a joint Gaussian process. **All settings are fixed in the code and were not tuned to any table** (fast mode uses a 100-row search, 100 trees, 100 boosting steps, 3 network members) | the approved plan |
| **How each model gives its 90% range:** mean baseline: mean plus or minus 1.645 training standard deviations; nearest neighbour: none; quadratic regression: plus or minus 1.645 times the spread of its training errors; boosted trees: split-conformal, with whole calibration groups (one group in four, or the latest fifth of the training dates) kept apart from the fitting rows, and no range if fewer than two groups can be spared; forest: spread between trees; network ensemble: spread between members; Gaussian process: its own predictive uncertainty | calibration is measured for every method separately; conformal is one candidate |
| **Joint versus independent fitting is stated in every report** (requested change 2): only the joint Gaussian process is fitted jointly across results (one shared set of length scales, trained on the rows that have every result); every other model, the ordinary Gaussian process included, is fitted independently per result. Every model row of every table says which, in words, and a check confirms it | not left implicit |
| **Folds now line up across results:** the splitter deals groups into folds using the rows of every modelled result, and each result keeps its own rows, so every result of a table has the same folds (needed for the joint model, and so a result's folds do not shift because of a few missing values). 2 more fold-plan checks | the joint model must not train on another result's test rows |
| **Selection rule as approved:** score = worst hold-out type (error divided by the result's spread); a model must beat both baselines in every hold-out type that can be run; ties within 5% go to the simpler or faster model; "no model beats the baseline" is a possible answer. Only the main comparison is judged: an exploratory comparison or a robustness-only table never gets a chosen model | requested rule, tested with six made-up cases |
| **Leakage alarm:** a held-out R-squared above 0.99 is reported as "CHECK FOR LEAKAGE" instead of being celebrated (message M42) | too good to be true usually is |
| **Labels in every heading** (requested): every heading of every model report and fold map carries the exploratory or robustness-only label when the table has one, and every line naming `api_code` carries "proxy, not a confirmed supplier" (checked line by line on the real reports) | the label cannot be missed |
| **Flagged rows with and without:** for the literature table the report shows every model's scores with the 118 flagged rows and again with those rows left out of both training and testing (the models are refitted) | requested; the flagged rows are kept in the main scores |
| **Fast mode** (`run_fast_validation.bat`, 6 folds at a time): all six tables in 3 min 12 s. Its numbers are a first look, not final. Reports go to `runs/modelling/` (not in git) | the stop you asked for before the long run |
| **Measured cost of the full run** (one slice, one fold at a time): about 6 s per fold and result on the batch data and about 12 s on the large mock (the Gaussian process about 8 s of that), against fast mode's 1.7 and 2.4 s. About 230 fold-and-result combinations over all tables gives roughly 40 to 60 minutes one at a time and about 10 to 20 minutes with 6 at a time. **This replaces the earlier estimate of about 3 hours (range 1.5 to 4.5), which was far too cautious**: the Gaussian process search converged much faster than my earlier test suggested. Still an estimate until the full run is done | stated before the long run |
| **Health check:** `check_models.bat` is now 76 checks and takes about 3.5 minutes, not the 2 minutes planned (it includes 200 random tables, fits on freshly generated mock data, and fast-mode reports on the real tables). It verifies: the selection rule on six made-up cases; known answers (an exact quadratic recovered to 0.7% of its spread; noise-free interpolation; a range that widens far from the data; conformal ranges that hit 0.896 against a 0.90 target); destroying the held-out answers changes no prediction (joint model included); the same predictions however many folds run at once; on a freshly generated seed, scored against the generator's true values with whole suppliers held out, every candidate beats the mean baseline by at least 10%; and the report's labels | the plan's self-tests |
| **Checks run for this step, per your instruction not to repeat them:** the modelling check (76), the setup-rules check (77) and the formats check (71) because the setup schema gained one field, and the report checks once more after a wording fix. The proof-of-concept stages, the ingest check and the architecture guard (inside the modelling check) were not re-run: nothing they cover changed | honest about what was and was not run |

**What the fast-mode results show (first look, not final; all numbers are error divided by the result's spread, lower is better, 1.0 is no better than guessing the mean):**
- **Mocks (synthetic):** every candidate beats the baselines; the quadratic regression is the rule's choice for dissolution and hardness on the easy mock (it suits a quadratic generator), and the neural-network ensemble for friability.
- **Batch data, main comparison (977 batches):** the boosted trees or random forest are chosen for all four results (hardness 0.36, tensile 0.45, dissolution 0.93, yield 0.85, worst hold-out type). Dissolution and yield are hard to predict (R-squared about 0.2 to 0.35); hardness and tensile much easier (about 0.8 to 0.9).
- **The quadratic regression fails badly on the two real tables** (error 8 to 10 times the spread on the batch data and 5 to 8 times on the literature table, R-squared from -2 to -93). Diagnosis on one fold: 902 squared and interaction terms for 781 training rows, a heavy-tailed table (held-out inputs up to 33 standard deviations from the training mean, which squaring magnifies about a thousand-fold), and a penalty of 1 that is too weak. Raising the penalty to 1,000 brings it to the mean baseline's level; a plain linear regression on the same fold scores 0.77, better than the tree models' 0.87. **Not changed**: any change to the model list or its settings needs your approval and, to stay honest, would be made knowing these results
- **Literature table (robustness only):** with whole APIs held out no model beats the mean baseline for hardness, friability or disintegration time. That is why it cannot support a learning-accuracy claim, and it is a real finding: these formulation records do not predict an API the model has not seen
- **Range hit rates:** the conformal ranges, the Gaussian process and the forest hit close to 90% in most tables; the neural-network ensemble's ranges hit only 45 to 60% (too narrow) and the quadratic regression's 7 to 30%. Flagged in the reports; judging and recalibrating belong to Stage 3b

### Changes requested before 3a-4, and Stage 3a-4 (the full comparison)
| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| **Label restored:** the extended batch comparison's label now reads "... missingness tied to one API source (confirmed in the source paper's Missing data section; every missing value is in the batches of a single API code), not part of the main comparison", in every heading | the project owner confirmed it from the paper itself |
| **Linear regression added as its own candidate, beside the quadratic one** (not instead of it). **Both choose their ridge penalty by validation strictly inside each training fold:** the training rows are re-divided into 5 folds of whole groups (or 3 forward-in-time blocks when the hold-out is by date), the penalty (from 0.01 to 10,000, a grid fixed in advance) with the smallest error on those inner groups is used, and the held-out fold never enters. The 90% range for both is plus or minus 1.645 times the error on those same inner groups, which is honest, unlike the training error. When fewer than two groups can be spared the penalty falls back to 1 and a message says so (it happened only on the small mock's supplier folds, where one supplier is left) | requested; the same kind of internal fitting already used for the Gaussian process |
| **Request 2a, answered honestly:** the quadratic regression **already** kept one-hot category columns out of the squared and interaction expansion (it expands only the numeric block; categories enter linearly). So the 902 terms of the failing fit were all from the 41 numeric inputs (41 squares and 861 pairwise products), not from categories, and this change could not shrink that count. It is now stated in the code and tested (5 numeric and 3 category columns give 20 expanded terms plus 3 linear, 23 in all) | the hypothesis was reasonable, but the code already did what was asked |
| **Request 2d: did the quadratic's error drop?** On the main batch comparison, yes: with whole API lots held out, error divided by spread went from 9.7, 1.8, 2.3 and 9.3 (dissolution, hardness, tensile, yield) to 0.97, 0.67, 0.79 and 1.26: from 5 to 10 times the spread down to roughly the level of guessing the mean. Over the worst of the three hold-out types the quadratic now scores 1.11, 0.79, 0.95 and 1.93. It is no longer catastrophic, but it is still not better than the plain linear regression (worst-type scores 0.97, 0.42, 0.55, 1.04), still fails to beat both baselines on all four results, and is still poor on the literature table (hardness 4.9 times the spread: about 2,300 squared and product terms for 1,982 rows). The quadratic is therefore never the rule's choice on the real tables | reported without spin: the fix removed the failure but the squares simply do not help on these tables |
| **Request 3, results that are not independent findings:** `tbl_yield` is now mapped and listed as left out with the reason "tbl_yield and batch_yield correlate at 0.95 (near-duplicates), so only batch_yield is modelled and the two must never be presented as independent findings". Only `batch_yield` is modelled, so the reports never show two yield results. Every report and the summary also get an automatic "Results that are not independent findings" section: tensile strength is calculated from hardness, so `tbl_tensile` and `tbl_av_hardness` move together | requested; the hardness and tensile pair really is a formula |
| **The full comparison is resumable:** each fold's answer is saved as soon as it finishes (`runs/modelling/cache_full/`, not in git), keyed on the data values, rows, inputs, settings and a code-version label, so a run that is stopped carries on where it stopped (checked: three missing and one damaged file are recomputed and the results are identical to an uninterrupted run); `--fresh` ignores the saved answers. **After changing any model code, change `CACHE_VERSION` in `validate.py` or run with `--fresh`** | the promise made in the plan |
| **New outputs:** `run_full_validation.bat` writes one report per table and **`model_summary_full.md`**, which keeps three parts apart: Part A the main comparisons (the only ones a model is chosen from), Part B the exploratory comparison (label in every heading, nothing chosen), Part C the robustness-only table (label in every heading, nothing chosen). Also `docs/modelling.md` (what it does and how to read it) and a README section | the plan's report, check and docs |
| **Measured: the full run took 15 min 37 s** with 6 folds at a time, against the revised estimate of 10 to 20 minutes. No model failed on any fold. **Then the ridge penalty search was made faster** (it now works through the smaller of the two Gram matrices instead of a full decomposition: the same mathematics, which a check proves by comparing it with scikit-learn's own ridge fitted penalty by penalty on a wide and a tall table), the cache version was bumped, and the whole comparison was **re-run: 9 min 16 s**, identical to the first run to three decimals with the same model chosen for every result. The reports in `runs/modelling/` are from this second run | an estimate checked against the real run; the speed-up verified, not assumed |
| **Checks for this step:** the modelling check (now 86 checks, 2 min 37 s) was run in full; the setup-rules (77) and formats (71) checks had been run after the schema change and nothing in them changed since. **Not re-run, because nothing they cover changed:** the proof-of-concept stages 1 to 5 and the ingest check | per your instruction not to repeat checks |

**What the full run shows (all labelled; error divided by the result's spread, worst of the hold-out types; 1.0 is no better than guessing the training mean):**
- **Synthetic mocks:** every candidate beats both baselines. The rule's choices are simple models: on the easy mock the quadratic regression (dissolution), the linear regression (hardness) and the neural-network ensemble (friability); on the hard mock the linear regression (dissolution and hardness) and the quadratic regression (friability). The mock generators are smooth, so smooth models do well; this says nothing about real tablets.
- **Real batch data, main comparison (977 batches):** the rule chooses the random forest for hardness (0.36), and the boosted trees for tensile strength (0.45), dissolution (0.93) and yield (0.85). **Hardness and tensile are predicted well; dissolution and yield are barely predictable from these inputs** across new API lots, the API-code stand-in for suppliers, and later months (the best models explain roughly 10 to 30% of the variation). Tensile strength is calculated from hardness, so those two are one finding, not two.
- **Exploratory batch comparison (633 batches):** nothing is chosen and nothing from it may be quoted as a result; the tables are there for information.
- **Literature table (robustness only):** with whole APIs held out, **no model beats the training mean for any of hardness, friability or disintegration time**, with or without the 118 flagged rows. A genuine negative result: these formulation records do not let a model predict for an API it has not seen. It also cannot support any claim about learning accuracy.
- **90% ranges:** on the larger tables the ranges of the two regressions (inner-group errors), the boosted trees (conformal) and the forest hit about 0.82 to 0.95 on held-out groups (the small mock's few groups give poorer ranges); the neural-network ensemble's ranges hit only about 0.33 to 0.74 (overconfident); the Gaussian processes vary from 0.65 to 0.99. Stage 3a reports them; judging and recalibrating belong to Stage 3b.

## Stage 3b (calibration, applicability-domain warning, decision-usefulness replay, scorecard): decisions, in the order they were made
Plan proposed 2026-10-11 and approved with two additions. Built in steps: 3b-1 calibration (**stop for review**), 3b-2 applicability-domain warning, 3b-3 decision-usefulness replay, 3b-4 scorecard, health check and docs (stop). No new libraries. **Status: DONE, tagged `stage3b-complete`** (the closing summary is at the end of this section). Like the 3a section, this is a record in the order decisions were made: where a later entry replaces an earlier one it says so.

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| **What is carried into 3b (fixed before any calibration number existed):** a result is **carried** if it is in a main comparison, its chosen model beats both baselines in every hold-out type, and its worst hold-out error is at most **0.70** of the result's spread; **carried but exploratory (borderline)** if the worst error is above 0.70 and at most **0.80**; **skipped** otherwise, or if the table is exploratory or robustness-only, or has fewer than 500 held-out rows. For every carried result the rule's choice and every model within its 5% tie are calibrated (22 model-and-result pairs, plus 5 borderline); the replay uses the rule's choice only | calibration numbers for a model that does not predict well enough would not matter |
| **Carried:** easy mock (synthetic): dissolution, hardness, friability; hard mock (synthetic): hardness, friability; real batch data, main comparison: hardness and tensile strength (tensile is calculated from hardness, so it is one finding with hardness). **Borderline, exploratory:** hard mock dissolution (the best model's worst error is 0.74; the line is judged on the best eligible model, not on the slightly weaker one the tie rule may prefer). **Skipped:** real batch dissolution (0.93; the best model explains about 13% of the variation in its hardest hold-out type) and batch yield (0.85; about 28%); every result on the literature table (no model beats the mean); the whole extended batch comparison; the small mock (89 rows, 2 to 4 groups) | from the 3a full run |
| **For the skipped results the scorecard says so and gives no calibration number:** "Calibration not assessed: the model does not predict well enough...", with the 3a numbers shown. **Consequence recorded:** dissolution, the cell's central target, cannot be validated on real data in 3b; calibration, the warning and the replay for dissolution are proven on the synthetic mocks only | honesty about what real data can support |
| **The decision-usefulness replay runs on the mocks only** (their targets are the proof-of-concept's own: dissolution at least 85, hardness 6 to 10 kP, friability at most 1.0). **It is not run on real batch hardness or tensile strength, and no invented specification window appears anywhere.** For those results the scorecard states plainly: "Decision-usefulness replay not run; no client-specified target exists for this result. The method is proven on synthetic data and can be applied here once real specification limits are available." | calibration and the warning test whether a stated number is honest, which is safe on real data; a replay simulates a decision outcome, and an invented window dressed as a target could later be read as a real result |
| **Scorecard (3b-4) must give the three statuses distinct visual treatment, not just a sentence:** "carried", "carried but exploratory (the 0.70 to 0.80 band)" and "skipped / not assessed" each get their own look (colour, marker and section), so a borderline result can never be mistaken for a main finding or an empty one for an assessed one. A scorecard check will confirm each status appears only in its own style | requested addition 1 |
| **Recalibration is computed entirely from the other folds, and a self-test proves it** (requested addition 2): artificially inflating one fold's errors leaves that fold's scale factor exactly unchanged (while another fold's factor does change, showing the test can see a difference). For forward-in-time hold-outs the factor uses only EARLIER steps, so a later step can never influence an earlier one | the guard must be provable |

### Stage 3b-1: calibration (built; stop for review)
`run_calibration.bat` reads the saved Stage 3a answers (it never refits a model) and writes `runs/modelling/calibration_summary.md` plus one report per table, in three separate parts: carried, carried but exploratory, and not assessed. `check_calibration.bat` is the health check (27 checks). Six new tested messages (41 in all).

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| **What is measured:** for each carried model-and-result pair and each hold-out type, how often the model's stated 90% range contained the truth on the held-out groups, with a 95% error bar from resampling whole held-out groups (none when there are fewer than 10 groups, as for 3 to 5 suppliers); the range's width as a share of the spread; and whether a wider stated range goes with a larger actual error, measured within each fold. Levels other than 90% are shown as the same range rescaled assuming bell-shaped errors and are never used for a verdict | a model states only a 90% range |
| **Verdict, fixed in advance:** honest if the 90% hit rate is 0.85 to 0.95 in every hold-out type; over-confident if below in any; under-confident if above in any; inconsistent if both | no verdict chosen after looking |
| **Recalibration** is a scale factor learned only from the OTHER folds' held-out errors; for forward-in-time hold-outs only EARLIER steps (so the first step has no factor and says so, M52). Proven by self-tests: inflating one fold's errors leaves that fold's factor exactly unchanged while every other fold's changes; replacing the fold's own entry with garbage changes nothing; a later step never changes an earlier one; and the same guard end to end through the full calibration | requested addition 2 |
| **Three problems found in my own work while reviewing the first output, all fixed before this review:** (1) the carry rule's "fewer than 500 held-out rows" test took the SMALLEST hold-out (the forward-in-time one tests only the later half, 418 rows), so real batch hardness and tensile strength were wrongly skipped; it now uses the largest hold-out, i.e. the table's size (977), restoring the list you approved; (2) the "knows when it is unsure" correlation was computed across folds, so a model stating one fixed range per fold got credit or blame that only came from folds differing from each other (negative numbers like -0.49 for the regressions); it is now measured within each fold and is "no: the same range for every recipe" where the range is fixed; (3) the carry line was judged on the tie-preferred model's score rather than the best model's (batch yield 0.88 instead of 0.85). A regression test covers each | honesty about my own errors |

**What calibration shows (27 model-and-result pairs: 22 carried and 5 borderline; all hold-out types; synthetic mocks unless stated):**
- **As stated, 12 honest, 10 over-confident, 5 under-confident.** By model: the neural-network ensemble is over-confident in all 3 of its pairs (hit rates 0.33 to 0.45); the quadratic regression 2 honest, 1 over, 2 under; the Gaussian process 3, 2, 1; the joint Gaussian process 2, 3, 1; the linear regression 2, 0, 1; the forest 2, 1, 0; the conformal boosted trees 1 honest.
- **Recalibration fixes a uniform over-confidence but does not transfer reliably.** Across the 22 carried pairs it moves the hit rate closer to 0.90 in 17 whole-batch hold-outs and none farther; in whole-supplier hold-outs closer in 15 and farther in 5; in forward-in-time hold-outs closer in 10 and farther in 9. The neural-network ensemble is made honest in all 3 pairs; a scale learned on other suppliers or earlier months can miss a new supplier or a later month.
- **Real batch data (labelled REAL PUBLIC DATA, NOT CLIENT DATA):** the random forest's ranges for hardness are honest as stated (hit rates 0.90, 0.87, 0.87 for whole lots, the API-code stand-in for suppliers, and later months); for tensile strength (one finding with hardness) the conformal boosted trees (0.91, 0.91, 0.89) and the forest (0.88, 0.86, 0.89) are honest as stated, but recalibrating the boosted trees makes the later-months hit rate worse (0.79).
- **Models that cannot say when they are unsure:** the linear, quadratic and conformal boosted-tree ranges are the same width for every recipe within a fold, so they cannot flag a recipe they are unsure about; the Gaussian processes' and the neural-network ensemble's ranges, although they differ from recipe to recipe, track the actual errors essentially not at all (rank correlation between -0.07 and 0.09 in every table), and only the forest's show a weak link (0.07 to 0.46, strongest on real tensile strength). So on these tables **no model flags the recipes it is least sure about**, which is exactly what 3b-2's applicability-domain warning has to supply.
- **Not assessed, no calibration number given (and listed with reasons):** real batch dissolution (best error 0.93 of the spread, about 13% explained) and yield (0.85, about 28%), all of the literature table, the extended batch comparison, and the small mock.
- **Checks run for this step:** the calibration check (27) and the modelling check (86, after one fix: my new calibration report's docstring spelled out the proxy phrase, which the one-place rule caught). Not re-run: the proof-of-concept stages, ingest, setup rules and formats (nothing they cover changed).

### Stage 3b-2: the applicability-domain warning ("is this recipe far from the data?"): design, fixed before any result existed
Accepted basis: the calibration results of 3b-1 (the verdict rule and the carry lines stay exactly as they were; nothing is adjusted to make a model look better).

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| **The warning is the platform's main safeguard right now, and the scorecard must surface it that way** (requested): 3b-1 found that no model's stated range tracks its own error (Gaussian processes and the neural ensemble: rank correlation between -0.07 and 0.09; the regressions and boosted trees state the same range for every recipe), so this warning is the only thing that can say "do not trust this prediction". The 3b-4 scorecard gives it its own prominent panel near the top, in plain words, stating that models do not flag their own unsureness and that this warning is the safeguard, together with how well it was shown to work | requested |
| **What it checks, in the space the models themselves work in** (numeric inputs standardised on the training rows, categories as 0/1 columns): (a) **distance:** the mean distance to the 5 nearest training rows; the threshold is the **95th percentile of the same distance for the training rows themselves, each measured only against training rows from OTHER groups** (leave-one-group-out, with the groups of the same kind as the hold-out being tested), so it mimics how far a new group normally sits; (b) **range:** any numeric input beyond the training range by more than **10% of that input's training range**, or a category never seen in training; a recipe is warned if (a) or (b) fires. 5 neighbours, the 95th percentile and the 10% tolerance are fixed now and not changed after seeing results | simple, explainable, and nothing to tune |
| **The threshold is set inside each training fold only**: it is computed from the fold's training rows alone, and a test proves that changing the held-out rows leaves the threshold identical while changing a training row changes it | the same guard as the recalibration factor |
| **Proof it works (each checked, none assumed):** on made-up data where the held-out groups come from the same source as the training groups, about 5% of rows are warned; recipes pushed 8 standard deviations away are warned essentially always; warned rows have larger errors than unwarned ones when errors really grow with distance, and the ratio is about 1 when they do not (a negative control); an unseen category is always warned and an excursion inside the 10% tolerance never is. On the real saved folds the same quantities are reported as findings, not asserted | requested proof-it-works checks |
| **Stability test (requested addition, reported beside the proof):** because the recalibration factor swung under a supplier or date shift (worse than raw in 5 of 22 carried pairs for suppliers and 9 of 22 for later dates), the warning's own threshold is compared across folds of each hold-out type: its largest-over-smallest ratio, set against the same ratio for the recalibration factors of the same folds, plus how much the share of held-out rows warned swings between folds. Criterion fixed in advance: a threshold ratio of at most 1.25 is called stable, up to 1.5 varying, above that swinging. The default reference groups are the hold-out's own kind; as a diagnostic the same numbers are also computed with the reference groups always taken to be batches, so that if the default swings we can see whether the definition is the cause | to know whether to trust the threshold under a supplier or date shift |
| **Which results:** only those carried or borderline in 3b-1 (7 carried, 1 borderline, marked exploratory); the warning uses the chosen model's saved held-out predictions only to measure whether warned rows have bigger errors. Skipped results get no warning evaluation | same rule as calibration |

### Stage 3b-2: the applicability-domain warning (built; stop for review)
`run_domain.bat` reads the saved Stage 3a answers and writes `runs/modelling/domain_summary.md` plus one report per table, in the same three separate parts as calibration (carried, carried but exploratory, not assessed), every report opening with the safeguard message. `check_domain.bat` is the health check (24 checks). Two new tested messages (43 in all): a threshold that could not be set on a fold, and a threshold that varies or swings.

**The stability question (asked before building): does the warning's own threshold swing under a supplier or date shift the way the recalibration factor did? No.** Across the 8 carried and borderline results, the largest over smallest distance threshold across the folds of a hold-out type is 1.01 to 1.12 for whole batches, 1.03 to 1.13 for suppliers and 1.04 to 1.08 for later dates: every one **stable** (limit 1.25), including real batch hardness and tensile (1.04 to 1.13). Measured against batches as the reference groups instead (the diagnostic) the ratios are 1.05 to 1.08, so the definition is not the reason. The recalibration factors of the same folds range up to 2.11 (suppliers) and 1.97 (later dates). The threshold is a property of the training inputs, which a held-out supplier does not change; the error scale, which the recalibration factor measures, is exactly what a shift changes.
