**The other proof-it-works checks, on made-up data with a known answer:** about 5% of rows from the same source as the training data are warned; recipes pushed 8 standard deviations away are warned essentially always (on the saved folds too: at least 95% in every hold-out type, 100% as rounded in the reports); warned rows have clearly larger errors when errors really grow with distance and about the same when they do not (negative control); the threshold uses the training rows alone (changing the held-out rows leaves it identical, changing training rows changes it) and equals a slow brute-force calculation that leaves out each row's own group; the range check ignores an excursion inside the 10% tolerance and catches one beyond it; an unseen category is always warned.

**What the warning does on the saved held-out folds (the carried results; findings, not asserted):**

| | Whole batches | Whole suppliers (stand-in on the real data) | Later dates |
|---|---|---|---|
| Rows warned by distance | 5% to 6% | 0% to 6% (median 1%) | 4% to 6% |
| Distance lift (share of the 10% largest errors it catches, over chance) | 1.0 to 2.85 (median 1.7; real hardness 2.1, tensile 2.9) | 0.0 to 2.85 (median 1.1) | 0.6 to 2.4 (median 1.4; real hardness 0.6, tensile 1.2) |
| Error ratio, warned over unwarned (distance) | 1.07 to 1.64 (median 1.26) | 1.07 to 1.30 | 0.94 to 1.45 |
| Rows warned by numeric range | 0% to 5% | 0% to 12% | 0% to 66% (real data 66%) |
| Rows warned for an unseen category | 0% to 1% | 100% | 0% |

**Honest reading.** The warning behaves as designed where the shift is among batches: it warns about 5% of rows and those rows carry two to three times their share of the largest errors on the real data. **Under a supplier or date shift it is much weaker:** its distance part barely moves (the mean distance in 40 to 70 equally weighted inputs hardly changes when a few inputs shift), so it warns about as many rows as it would anyway and, for real hardness under later dates, does worse than chance; its range part warns 66% of real rows under later dates (process measurements drift) without picking out the bad ones (lift 0.84 to 1.09); and its unseen-category part warns every row under a supplier hold-out, which is true (the held-out supplier's identity is itself an input value the model never saw) but leaves nothing to compare. So for a new supplier or a later month the warning can say "this is a new group", but it cannot yet rank which recipes inside it are the least trustworthy.

**Not changed, and proposed for your decision (a change after seeing these results must be judged on fresh data and the same proof set, never by tuning to these tables):** (1) report the three checks separately in the scorecard and give "unseen supplier or product" its own status instead of folding it into one flag; (2) make the range check less jumpy, for example by requiring several inputs out of range or a wider tolerance, since 66% on real later dates is not a usable warning; (3) measure distance on the inputs the models rely on most rather than on all of them equally, since equal weighting dilutes a shift in a few inputs; (4) treat a supplier or date shift as a group-level event (is this whole batch of recipes new?) in addition to a per-recipe one.

**Checks run for this step:** the domain check (24) and the modelling check (the message catalogue and its document changed). Not re-run: the calibration check (nothing it covers changed), the proof-of-concept stages, ingest, setup rules and formats.

### Open decision, parked on purpose: the four options for the warning's weakness under supplier and date shifts
**Status (updated after the demo review): DECIDED, see "Decision on the four options" below.** Earlier status: open, pending review after the demo. Decided 2026-10-11 (evening before the demo): nothing in 3b-2's warning is changed, none of the four options is built, and all modelling work, including Stage 3b-3 (the decision-usefulness replay), is stopped until after the demo. The four options are recorded under "Stage 3b-2" above: (1) report the three checks separately with "unseen supplier or product" as its own status; (2) make the range check less jumpy; (3) measure distance on the inputs the models rely on most; (4) treat a supplier or date shift as a group-level event. Until decided, the warning stays exactly as built and its limits are stated plainly (strong for new batches; under a supplier or date shift it can say "this is a new group" but cannot rank the recipes inside it). The demo presents this as an honest finding, not a defect to hide. Stage 3b stays IN PROGRESS: 3b-1 and 3b-2 are committed and awaiting review; 3b-3 and 3b-4 are not started. **(That was the position on the evening before the demo; everything in this sentence has since been decided, built and tagged, see below.)**

### Decision on the four options for the warning's weakness (made after the demo): two built, one declined, one deferred
| Option | Decision | Reasoning |
|---|---|---|
| **1. Report the three per-recipe checks as separate statuses, with "unseen supplier or product" as its own explicit status** | **BUILD (part of 3b-2)** | A composite "warned" flag hid which check fired and made a supplier shift (where the unseen-category check fires on every row) look like everything is warned. There is no composite headline any more: the summary and the scorecard show distance, numeric range and unseen supplier or product each as its own status, each with its own warned share and its own evidence, and name the unseen values. |
| **2. Make the range check less jumpy (looser tolerance, or several inputs out of range)** | **DECLINED for now; open to revisit with a design fixed in advance and tested on fresh data** | The 66% warned under later dates on the real data is exactly the result that would be driving the change, which is after-the-fact tuning of the kind the project avoids everywhere else. The range check keeps its tolerance of 10% of the training range. |
| **3. Measure distance on the most influential inputs only** | **CONSIDERED AND DEFERRED to a later stage (not rejected)** | A legitimate idea, but it risks circularity: using a model's own feature importances to decide where that same model can be trusted means the trust region is shaped by the model it is meant to check. It would need an importance measure that does not come from the model being judged, and a design fixed in advance. |
| **4. A group-level warning, separate from the per-recipe one: "is this whole batch of recipes from an unfamiliar supplier, product or time period?"** | **BUILD properly (part of 3b-2)**, alongside and not replacing the per-recipe checks | A supplier or date shift is a property of a whole group, and a group has far more statistical power than any one recipe. |

**The group-level method, fixed before any result or test exists:**
- **Unit:** a group of recipes that arrive together: a batch, a supplier, or a block of dates (a time period). In evaluation, each held-out batch and each held-out supplier is one group; the held-out block of a forward-in-time step is one group. Groups of fewer than 5 rows are not assessed (too small to say anything) and are counted.
- **Statistic:** in the space the models work in (numeric inputs standardised on the training rows, categories as 0/1 columns), T = n x (squared length of the difference between the group's mean and the training rows' mean) / p, where n is the group's rows and p the number of encoded inputs. Under no shift T is about 1; a shift in a few inputs raises it in proportion to n.
- **Threshold: the 95th percentile of the same T for groups that are known to be ordinary.** For each training group of the same kind with at least n rows, draw 10 random subsets of n of its rows and measure T against the training rows OUTSIDE that group (so ordinary between-group differences, such as batch effects, are inside the threshold). This needs at least 10 such training groups. **With fewer (suppliers: 2 to 4) the threshold is the 95th percentile of T for random subsets of n training rows, which knows nothing about how much groups normally differ; it is labelled "compared with chance alone" and is expected to flag groups more readily.** Fixed values: 95th percentile, 10 draws per null group, 500 chance draws, seed 20261012.
- **Explanation:** the three inputs that moved most (shift in standard errors, with sign) are named.
- **Its own steadiness is reported beside the per-recipe threshold:** the threshold for a group of 30 rows on every fold, largest over smallest, by the same 1.25 and 1.5 lines.
- **Proof, on known-answer synthetic scenarios fixed now:** (S1) groups from the same source are flagged about 5% of the time; (S2) a group shifted by 1 standard deviation in 3 of 40 inputs (30 rows) is flagged at least 90% of the time while the per-recipe distance check flags at most 12% of its rows; (S3) a group with an unseen category is flagged by the unseen-category status in every row; (S4) a later time period drifting by 0.5 standard deviations in 10 of 40 inputs (60 rows) is flagged at least 90% of the time; **(S5, new and specific to group detection)** (a) small groups of 5 rows with the S2 shift: power is reported and must lie below that of 30-row groups and above the false-alarm rate; (b) groups with a large ordinary batch effect and no shift: flagged about 5% of the time with the group-based threshold while a chance-only threshold flags them far more often (the reason the group-based threshold exists); (c) fewer than 10 training groups: the chance-only label is used and shown. On the saved folds the same quantities are reported as findings, not asserted, and any limit it turns out to have is reported plainly.
- **The scorecard (3b-4) must show four separate warning statuses:** distance, numeric range, unseen supplier or product, and group unfamiliar; no composite.

### Stage 3b-2, second pass: four separate warning statuses and the group-level warning (built; stop for review before 3b-3)
`run_domain.bat` now reports **four separate statuses** for every carried result and hold-out type, with no combined "warned" flag anywhere: (1) per recipe, distance; (2) per recipe, numeric range; (3) per recipe, **unseen supplier or product** (a category value never seen, with the values named); (4) **whole group**: is this batch, supplier or time period unfamiliar. `check_domain.bat` is now 38 checks. Two more tested messages (45 in all). The range check's tolerance is unchanged (option 2 declined) and no influential-inputs distance exists (option 3 deferred).

**Item 1, separate statuses (real batch data):** under the API-code hold-out the unseen-supplier-or-product status warns every recipe and names what was never seen (product codes 17, 23, 13 and 1, with 195, 187, 117 and 95 rows): the model has never seen those products. Distance and range are reported beside it with their own error ratios and lift. The scorecard (3b-4) must show the same four statuses.

**Item 4, the group-level warning, against the criteria fixed beforehand (all met; nothing was tuned):**

| Scenario | Criterion fixed in advance | Measured |
|---|---|---|
| S1 groups from the same source | flagged about 5% | 4.5% (200 groups) |
| S2 30 recipes shifted by 1 standard deviation in 3 of 40 inputs | group flagged at least 90%; per-recipe distance at most 12% of its rows | group 99%; per-recipe distance 9.5% of rows (nearly blind) |
| S3 a group from a never-seen supplier | the unseen-supplier-or-product status flags every recipe and names it | 100%, named |
| S4 a later period, 0.5 standard deviations of drift in 10 of 40 inputs, 60 recipes | at least 90% | 100% |
| S5a groups of only 5 recipes | weaker than for 30, stronger than the false-alarm rate | 42% (30 recipes: 99%; false alarm 6%) |
| S5b a large ordinary batch effect, no shift | group-based threshold about 5%; chance-alone far more | 3% against 100% |
| S5c fewer than 10 training groups | the "chance alone" label and its message | shown |

Guards (each tested): a group's verdict does not depend on which other groups are checked; the threshold comes from the training rows alone; a group under 5 recipes is counted, not assessed; the inputs that moved most are named with their direction.

**What it does on the saved held-out folds (findings, not asserted):**
- **Where there are enough training groups to learn how much groups normally differ** (held-out batches; 448 group checks across the carried results): **5% flagged**, exactly the designed false-alarm rate. Flagged batches are only slightly worse predicted (error ratio 1.06 to 1.19; rank correlation across batches -0.16 to 0.20). Batches of the same process that look slightly unusual are not noticeably harder to predict.
- **Its real limit, reported plainly:** a supplier or time period has only 2 to 5 training groups to learn from, far fewer than the 10 needed, so the threshold falls back to **chance alone** and **every** held-out supplier (28 checks, 3 to 5 suppliers per table) and time period (32 checks) is flagged: 100%. It is true that each is a new group, but the warning cannot yet say whether a particular new supplier or period is unusually different. The same fallback flagged 94% of 70 checks on larger held-out batches where too few training batches were big enough. S5b shows why: without a group-based threshold, ordinary group-to-group differences look like shifts.
- **The group threshold for 30 recipes is steady across folds** (largest over smallest 1.01 to 1.18 across all tables and hold-outs).

**What would address the limit (not built; a change needs its own design fixed in advance and testing on fresh data):** finer time blocks (single dates or months) as the ordinary-group reference for time periods, so there are enough of them; for suppliers there is no fix from three to five suppliers, and the honest statement is that a new supplier can only be judged unfamiliar by the unseen-supplier-or-product status and by expert review.

**Checks run for this step:** the domain check (38) and the modelling check (the message catalogue and its document changed). Not re-run: the calibration check, the proof-of-concept stages, ingest, setup rules and formats (nothing they cover changed).

### Approved after review of 3b-2, and one statement that ties three findings together (requested 2026-10-11)
Items 1 and 4 of the four options are approved as built, including the group warning's honestly reported limit (it falls back to "chance alone" below 10 training groups).

**The unifying statement. It belongs in this file and, word for word, on the 3b-4 scorecard near the top, next to the warning panel:**

> **Everything built so far recognizes batch-level variation well, but struggles specifically when the shift is a new supplier or a later time period.**

It is one finding seen three ways, not three separate problems:
1. **Recalibration (3b-1):** a scale factor learned on other batches fixes a model's stated range for new batches (closer to 90% in 17 of 22 carried pairs, farther in none), but does not transfer reliably to a new supplier (farther in 5 of 22) or a later month (farther in 9 of 22).
2. **The per-recipe distance check (3b-2):** picks out the large errors under whole-batch shifts (real hardness 2.1 times chance, tensile 2.9), but under the real data's later-date shift it is not reliably better than chance (real hardness 0.6 times chance, tensile 1.2; "worse than chance" for hardness, "about chance" for tensile).
3. **The group warning (3b-2, second pass):** with only 2 to 5 training suppliers or time periods it cannot learn how much groups normally differ, so it falls back to "chance alone" and flags every new supplier (28 of 28) and every later period (32 of 32), which cannot discriminate; with enough training batches it flags the designed 5% (448 checks).

The common cause: a model and its safeguards learn how much things vary from the groups they have seen. Batches are plentiful, so batch-level variation is well learned. A new supplier or a later period is a kind of variation the training data has only a handful of examples of, so nothing built so far can say how far it matters.

### Does the weakness show on the mocks too? (checked before building 3b-3; asked 2026-10-11)
Measured on the saved held-out folds of the six carried mock results (hard mock dissolution is the borderline one), nothing refitted, per-recipe checks only:

| Hold-out type | Rows warned by distance (of about 1,300 to 2,900) | Distance lift (1.0 = chance) | Error ratio, warned over unwarned | Numeric range | Unseen category |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Whole batches | 124 to 147 (about 5%) | 1.00 to 2.27 (median 1.45) | 1.07 to 1.42 | 0 rows warned | 0% |
| Later dates | 62 to 90 (5% to 6%) | 1.16 to 2.42 (median 1.56) | 1.11 to 1.45 | 0 rows warned | 0% |
| Whole suppliers | 3 (easy mock) or 28 (hard mock) | 0.00 to 2.85 (median 0.53), from 3 to 28 rows: **cannot be judged** | 1.07 to 1.30, only where at least 10 rows were warned (hard mock) | 0 rows warned | 100%, by construction (the held-out supplier is itself a new value) |

**Answer: partly the same weakness, partly specific to the real data, and the mocks cannot show the part that matters most.**
- **Supplier shift: the same weakness, for a different reason.** In `tools/make_mock_data.py` a supplier changes the measured result, not the recipe, and the recipe inputs are drawn without regard to the supplier (the supplier explains at most 0.2% of any numeric input's spread; on the real data the API code explains up to 86% of one). A check that looks only at recipe inputs cannot see a shift that is not in them, so the distance check warns almost nothing (3 and 28 rows) and its lift is meaningless at that size.
- **Later-date shift: not the real data's failure, but only because the mocks have no input drift.** On the mocks the distance check picks out the large errors under later dates about as well as under batches (lift 1.16 to 2.42, never below chance). But in the mocks the recipe inputs do not move with the date (largest rank correlation of any input with the date: 0.03; on the real batch data 13 of 41 inputs exceed 0.3, largest 0.55). The mocks' date shift is a drift in the instrument and the results, which no input check can see. So the mocks are the **easy case** for later dates, and a good date result on them says nothing about the real data's input drift, where the warning did no better than chance.
- **Numeric range: silent on the mocks everywhere** (every recipe lies inside the training range), so it cannot be assessed there. On the real data it warned 66% under later dates without picking out the bad rows.

**What this means for the replay (decided now, before it is built).** You gave two acceptable answers: restrict the cutoffs to batch-type splits, or state plainly when the replay's own risk-flagging is unreliable (supplier, date) and when it is reliable (batch), never one number that hides which kind of shift it holds for. **The replay takes the second.** The first is not possible: a replay is forward in time by construction, so every cutoff is a later-period shift. This does not change the design already approved (mocks only, a replay that never sees the future, no invented window, results kept by hold-out type); it makes the reporting rule binding:
- The replay reports its warned-versus-unwarned evidence **only for the shift it actually is (later dates)** and says that the same warning's whole-batch and whole-supplier evidence comes from the saved 3b-2 hold-outs on the same mock results, shown beside it, never merged into one lift.
- Every replay report states, in these words or equivalent, what the mocks cannot show: that recipe inputs neither drift with the date nor differ by supplier in them, so the replay's date and supplier evidence is the easy case and says nothing about real input drift or a real supplier change.
- A shift type is called **"can be judged"** only when at least 30 rows were warned (a count that does not depend on any result) and **"picks out the large errors"** only when the lift is above 1 (chance) in every result of that shift type; **"mixed"** when above 1 in some; **"does not"** when in none. Fewer than 30 warned rows is "cannot be judged" and no lift is stated as a finding. Both lines are the natural ones (a minimum sample and the chance line), set now and not tuned. The 3b-2 numbers in the table above were seen before these lines were written; neither line was chosen to produce a particular word.

### Stage 3b-3: the decision-usefulness replay: design, fixed before any replay number existed (mocks only)
**The question:** if the cell had used a model to choose which recipes to run next, would it have chosen better than chance, and how far from perfect? **Synthetic mocks only** (carried or borderline results of the easy and hard mocks). It is **not** run on real batch hardness or tensile strength (no client-specified target exists), the small mock is skipped, and no invented window appears anywhere.

| Decision | Why |
|---|---|
| **A decision window is one forward step of the Stage 3a later-dates hold-out** (4 steps per mock result; each step trains on every row dated before its block and is tested on the block). The replay reuses those saved predictions and **refits nothing**, so its numbers are the 3a numbers read a new way. Each window is checked again before use: no training row may share a position with, or be dated on or after, any candidate; a planted leak must be refused | the model at each decision only knows the past |
| **Targets are read from the setup, not typed:** dissolution at least 85, hardness 6 to 10 kP, friability at most 1.0 (the proof of concept's own, in the mock setups). A candidate **passes** when its measured value meets the target; **all three** pass only when all three were measured on that row and all met | no invented window |
| **How candidates are ranked:** by the model's own probability that the target is met, using the rule's chosen model for that result, its predicted value and its **stated 90% range as stated (not recalibrated: 3b-1 showed recalibration does not transfer reliably)**, taking the range as a bell-shaped spread (standard deviation = range width ÷ 3.29). For all three together the three probabilities are multiplied (assumes the three results are independent, which they are not; this is the same joint-versus-independent limitation every report states). Nothing measured in the window is used | uses only what the model said before the window |
| **Pick size: the top 10% of each window's candidates**, ties broken by position (nothing random) | fixed now |
| **Compared with:** random picking (the window's own pass rate, exact, no sampling) and the perfect ranking (all passers first: the smaller of 100% and passers divided by picks). **Captured share = (picked − random) ÷ (perfect − random)** | the user's requested comparison |
| **Error bar:** 95% interval for (picked rate − random rate) from resampling whole batches within each window (2000 draws, seed 20261013), pooled over the four windows | rows of one batch are not independent |
| **Verdict, fixed in advance:** "beat random" if the pooled picked rate is above the random rate and the interval's lower end is above 0; "did not beat random" otherwise; **"no room to improve"** (and no verdict) if the random rate is at least 98%, or perfect minus random is under 2 percentage points | friability is met by more than 99% of mock recipes, so ranking for it is meaningless and is said to be |
| **Results reported:** each target on its own, and all three together. The all-three result on the hard mock is labelled EXPLORATORY: borderline (it contains the borderline dissolution result), as is hard mock dissolution on its own | same labelling rule as 3b-1 and 3b-2 |
| **Did warned picks do worse?** For each per-recipe status separately (distance, numeric range, unseen category), the pass rate of warned picks against unwarned picks, **only when at least 30 picks were warned**; otherwise "too few warned picks to say", with the count. Reported for later dates only, with the reliability statement above beside it | no composite flag; no claim the evidence cannot carry |
| **Proof it works, on known answers, fixed now:** a predictor that knows the truth picks the perfect ranking's pass rate; a predictor that says the same thing for every recipe gets the random rate; a predictor that is exactly backwards gets less than random; changing the measured outcomes of a window changes the pass rates but never which recipes are picked; a planted leak is refused | each guard and each statistic is tested |
| **Messages:** two new tested messages (M70 target with no room to improve, M71 too few warned picks) | one test per message |
| **Scorecard (3b-4) must carry,** for results it does not replay (real batch hardness and tensile): "Decision-usefulness replay not run; no client-specified target exists for this result. The method is proven on synthetic data and can be applied here once real specification limits are available." | already decided; recorded again so the scorecard step cannot forget it |

### Stage 3b-3: the decision-usefulness replay (built; stop for review before 3b-4)
`run_replay.bat` reads the saved Stage 3a answers (it refits nothing; about 30 seconds) and writes `runs/modelling/replay_summary.md` plus one report per mock. `check_replay.bat` is the health check (43 checks; it is also part of `check_all.bat`). Two new tested messages (47 in all). The design above was built exactly as fixed; nothing in it was changed after the first numbers were seen.

**What the replay shows (synthetic mocks, four forward-in-time windows each, top 10% picked; pass rate of the picks, against random picking and the perfect ranking):**

| Mock and target | Picked | Random | Perfect | 95% interval for the gain (points) | Verdict |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Easy: dissolution at least 85 | 98.6% | 28.3% | 100% | +63.7 to +74.8 | beat random |
| Easy: hardness 6 to 10 kP | 100% | 74.1% | 100% | +24.1 to +27.6 | beat random |
| Easy: friability at most 1.0 | 100% | 99.6% | 100% | +0.0 to +0.9 | **no room to improve** (no verdict) |
| Easy: all three together | 93.6% | 21.2% | 100% | +67.3 to +75.4 | beat random |
| Hard: hardness 6 to 10 kP | 100% | 76.8% | 100% | +21.0 to +25.6 | beat random |
| Hard: friability at most 1.0 | 100% | 99.6% | 100% | +0.1 to +0.7 | **no room to improve** (no verdict) |
| Hard: dissolution at least 85 (**EXPLORATORY: borderline**) | 89.7% | 23.5% | 100% | +55.2 to +74.0 | beat random |
| Hard: all three together (**EXPLORATORY: contains the borderline result**) | 81.5% | 19.8% | 100% | +50.6 to +70.0 | beat random |

**Honest reading.** On these synthetic mocks a model's own probabilities pick far better recipes than chance, and on the easy mock they capture 92% to 100% of what a perfect ranking could (77% to 87% on the hard mock's exploratory results). This is the best case, for three reasons that every report states: the picks are the 10% of recipes the model was most sure of (well inside the target, not near the margin); the mocks are easy to rank; and a pick is a measured row (repeat measurements of one recipe, about 1.2 rows per recipe in these mocks, count separately). Friability is met by more than 99% of mock recipes anyway, so ranking cannot improve on random picking and the report says "no room to improve" rather than giving a verdict.

**Did warned picks do worse? Not answerable here, and the reports say so.** Only 0 to 18 of the 130 to 144 picks were warned by any one per-recipe status, always fewer than the 30 needed, so no comparison of warned and unwarned picks is made (message M71). The picks are the recipes the model is most sure of, which are also the recipes nearest the training data.

**The warning by kind of shift (the requirement from your review, shown beside the replay and never merged):**

| Mock | Whole batches | Whole suppliers | Later dates (the replay's own shift) |
|---|---|---|---|
| Easy | **mixed** (lift 1.37, 1.15, 1.00; 140 to 147 rows warned) | **cannot be judged** (3 rows warned) | **picks out the large errors** (lift 1.68, 1.44, 1.16; 86 to 90 rows) |
| Hard | **picks out the large errors** (1.79, 2.27, 1.53; 124 to 132 rows) | **cannot be judged** (28 rows warned) | **picks out the large errors** (1.77, 2.42, 1.38; 62 to 66 rows) |

Each report adds the unifying statement and the plain paragraph on what the mocks cannot show (their inputs neither move with the date, largest rank correlation 0.03, nor differ by supplier, at most 0.2% of an input's spread), so a good later-dates reading here is the easy case and says nothing about real input drift or a real supplier change.

**Guards, each tested:** a predictor that knows the truth gets the perfect pass rate; one that says the same for every recipe gets about the random rate and no 'beat random' verdict; one that is exactly backwards does worse than random; the verdict, 'no room' and 'cannot be judged' lines sit exactly where they were fixed; planted leaks (a training row from inside the window, a training row dated after the window starts, a row in both) are found, and a leak planted in the real saved folds makes the replay refuse to run; replacing every measured value in the windows changes which recipes passed but not one probability and not one pick; the replay refuses any table not labelled synthetic; real batch hardness and tensile strength get the fixed sentence and no window of any kind.

**Three slips of mine, found and fixed before this review:** (1) the first run of my own check failed twice: a report heading without the data-status label, and a check that treated the harmless word "combined" as a combined flag; (2) the report for the small mock, where nothing was replayed, showed an empty warning table and still made a claim about the real data (now left out); (3) I wrote that the distance check "did no better than chance" under the real data's later dates, which overstates it for tensile strength (1.2 times chance, hardness 0.6): corrected here and in the reports to "not reliably better than chance".

**Checks run for this step:** the replay check (43) and the modelling check (86, because the message catalogue and its document changed). Not re-run: calibration, domain, the proof-of-concept stages, ingest, setup rules and formats (nothing they cover changed). (Superseded: 3b-4, the scorecard with its health check and docs, was built next and Stage 3b is now complete and tagged; see below.)

### Approved after review of 3b-3, with two additions (2026-10-11)
3b-3 is approved as built.

**Addition 1: a known limitation of the test fixtures themselves (the scorecard must carry it too, as its own item, not inside the general "the method is proven on synthetic data" story).** The mock generator's supplier effect barely touches the recipe inputs: the supplier explains at most **0.2% of any input's spread** on the mocks, against **86%** on the real batch data (the API-code stand-in, `api_code`). So **the mocks cannot and do not validate the distance check's behaviour under a supplier shift.** The "cannot be judged" reading for whole suppliers on the mocks (3 or 28 rows warned, under the 30-row line) is **a gap in the design of the synthetic test, not a neutral or a passing result**. The same gap applies to later dates for input drift: the mocks' inputs do not move with the date (largest rank correlation 0.03, against 13 of 41 real inputs above 0.3), so a good later-dates reading on the mocks is the easy case. Closing the gap would need a mock generator in which a supplier or a period changes the recipes' inputs; that is a separate piece of work, not done and not promised here.

**Addition 2: the per-shift-type verdict rule is evaluated for each result separately and is never averaged.** Confirmed from the code (`reliability_word` in `cellpoc/modelling/replay.py`): the "at least 30 warned rows" test is applied to every result's own count (the smallest decides), and "picks out the large errors" needs the lift to be above 1 **in every result**. One result at 1.2 cannot carry another at 0.6. The words are: "picks out the large errors" (every result above 1), "mixed" (some above, some not), "does not" (none above), "cannot be judged" (a result has fewer than 30 warned rows or no lift). **Only the first is a "reliable" reading; the other three are all "not reliable", and the scorecard must treat them that way.**

**Worked example on the real batch data (REAL PUBLIC DATA, NOT CLIENT DATA; saved Stage 3a answers, nothing refitted; hardness and tensile strength carried in 3b-1):**

| Kind of shift | Rows warned by distance (hardness, tensile) | Lift (hardness, tensile) | Error ratio, warned over unwarned | What the fixed rule says |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Whole batches | 56, 56 | 2.14, 2.85 | 1.52, 1.64 | **picks out the large errors** |
| Whole API codes (the supplier stand-in; *proxy, not a confirmed supplier*) | 54, 54 | 2.03, 1.48 | 1.09, 1.28 | **picks out the large errors** |
| Later dates | 17, 17 | **0.59, 1.17** | 0.94, 0.94 | **cannot be judged** (17 rows is under the 30-row line) |

Three things this shows, reported as they came out:
1. **Hardness 0.6 and tensile 1.2 under later dates are not rated "reliable"**, and not because of an average (the average would be 0.88; it is never computed): the rule refuses to judge at all, because only 17 rows were warned. With 30 or more warned rows the same two lifts would be **"mixed"**, which is "not reliable". Tensile alone clearing the bar can never produce "reliable".
2. **Hardness and tensile are one finding** (tensile is calculated from hardness): both results are warned on exactly the same rows, so "two results" is really one set of rows seen twice. The scorecard must say so rather than present them as two independent confirmations.
3. **A limit of the fixed rule, found while doing this: it uses the lift only, so "picks out the large errors" can be a generous word.** For whole API codes both lifts are above 1 (2.03 and 1.48), so the rule says "picks out the large errors", yet warned rows' errors are only 1.09 and 1.28 times unwarned rows'. This sits oddly beside the unifying statement, which rests on recalibration and the group warning, not on this per-recipe check. The rule is **not changed** (a change after seeing these numbers would be tuning); the scorecard therefore must **never show the word alone**: every reading is shown with each result's rows warned, lift and error ratio beside it.

### Stage 3b-4: the model scorecard (built; stop for review before `stage3b-complete` is tagged)
`run_scorecard.bat` reads the saved Stage 3a answers (nothing refitted; about a minute) and writes `runs/scorecard/index.html` plus one stand-alone page per table (six). Plain HTML and CSS: no script, no library, no web address, Deloitte palette only, Open Sans with an Arial fallback. `check_scorecard.bat` is the health check (39 checks; also part of `check_all.bat`). No new message was needed (the catalogue stays at 47).

**Built as planned:** each page has the data-status banner (and any exploratory or robustness label), your unifying statement word for word, the safeguard panel with the four statuses listed separately (no combined flag), a table of readings per kind of shift (each result's rows warned, lift and error ratio beside the word, the whole-group warning beside it), the test-data limitation as its own panel on synthetic pages and the index, the results by status, the replay on the synthetic mocks, and "what this does not show". Real batch hardness and tensile strength carry the fixed "replay not run" sentence and no window. Every mention of `api_code` carries the proxy phrase. The index links the six pages, explains the three statuses and four readings, and repeats the unifying statement and the limitation.

**Your addition to the check, built:** the three "not reliable" readings (weak but positive, not enough data, clearly negative) each have their own look, and so does the one reliable reading: different backgrounds, borders and markers (green solid with a triangle for "picks out the large errors"; light blue with a double border and a half-filled circle for "mixed"; white with a dotted border and a question mark for "cannot be judged"; dark with a solid border and a cross for "does not"). The check requires that any two readings differ in at least two of background, border and marker, and that the three not-reliable ones differ in all three; it also checks that carried, carried-but-exploratory and not-assessed results have three different border styles and appear only in their own style.

**Added after your approval review (confirmation asked before tagging): every carried result's card shows all four warning statuses.** My first check only confirmed the four statuses in the safeguard panel, not in each card, so the honest answer to your question was "not yet". The check now tests every carried and borderline result's card on every page (8 of them): the distance, numeric range, unseen supplier or product and whole-group columns are all there, there is one row per hold-out type the result has, and each row carries its figures. A negative control shows the same test does report a card with a column removed. (My first version of this test paired cards with results in the wrong order, because the page groups cards by status; it was a bug in the test, not in the pages, and is fixed.)

**Two choices of mine, for your review:** (1) a borderline result (hard mock dissolution) is included in the readings it contributes to, as the replay already did, and is marked EXPLORATORY inside them, rather than hidden; (2) the readings table uses the per-recipe distance check only (the three statuses and the whole-group warning are all shown separately in each result's card, and the whole-group result sits beside each reading).

**Checks run for this step:** the scorecard check (37 at first, 39 after the per-card status test described above; after fixing two bugs in my own check: it removed the space from "Open Sans" before comparing, and compared a raw apostrophe with the page's escaped one). Not re-run since the replay step: the replay check, modelling, calibration, domain, the proof-of-concept stages, ingest, setup rules and formats (the new files passed the same random-splitter and architecture scans inside the scorecard check). Stage 3b is complete in the code but **not tagged**: tagging `stage3b-complete` waits for your review.

### Stage 3b: complete (tag `stage3b-complete`, 2026-10-11): where things stand
**Built and approved:** 3b-1 calibration with a carry rule fixed in advance (7 carried results, 1 exploratory, the rest not assessed with reasons); 3b-2 the applicability-domain warning with four separate statuses and no combined flag; 3b-3 the decision-usefulness replay on the synthetic mocks only; 3b-4 the scorecard (one offline page per table plus an index, `run_scorecard.bat`). Health checks added in 3b: calibration (27), domain (38), replay (43), scorecard (39); the modelling check is 86 and the modelling message catalogue has 47 messages. `check_all.bat` runs every check, including these four. No client data was used anywhere; every output is labelled synthetic or real public data.

**The finding behind the whole stage (on every scorecard page):** everything built so far recognizes batch-level variation well, but struggles specifically when the shift is a new supplier or a later time period.

**Open items carried forward (recorded so none is forgotten; none is a plan for Stage 4):**
- **The sealed acceptance lab (Stage A): was not written when this summary was made; it was created and sealed afterwards, on 2026-10-11** (see "The sealed acceptance lab" below). The plan to write it before modelling began was not kept.
- **The test data cannot validate the warning under a supplier shift or input drift** (the mock generator's supplier effect barely touches the inputs: at most 0.2% of an input's spread, against 86% on the real data). A generator in which a supplier or a period changes the recipes' inputs would be needed; not built, not promised.
- **Dissolution, the cell's central target, cannot be validated on real data:** real batch dissolution and yield are barely predictable and were not carried; calibration, the warning and the replay for dissolution are shown on the synthetic mocks only. The replay was never run on real batch hardness or tensile strength (no client-specified target exists).
- **Four warning options:** two built, one declined (a looser range check: open to revisit with a design fixed in advance and tested on fresh data), one deferred (distance on the most influential inputs only, to avoid circularity). A finer time-block reference for the group warning (to give time periods enough ordinary groups to compare with) is also not built.
- **The scorecard's reading uses lift only,** so "picks out the large errors" can be a generous word; the pages always print each result's rows warned, lift and error ratio beside it. The rule was not changed after seeing the numbers.

### The sealed acceptance lab (created and sealed 2026-10-11, tag `sealed-lab-v1`)
Requested before any Stage 4 planning: the blocker recorded in `docs/platform_plan.md` is removed by creating the lab now. It lives in `acceptance/` (outside `cellpoc/`, so no part of the running platform can import it by accident), with its own check, `check_seal.bat` (also part of `check_all.bat`).

**What it is.** A second synthetic lab, `acceptance/sealed_lab.py`, whose truth is structurally different from the development lab and from the mock generator: dissolution is a Weibull release mechanism driven by porosity, disintegrant swelling, binder and lubricant film; hardness follows a Ryshkewitch law (strength falls exponentially with porosity); friability depends on true hardness, over-lubrication and lamination at high force, so it is a real constraint (the mock generator's friability passes over 99% of the time); batch and supplier effects act on latent parameters, not as offsets; noise is heteroscedastic. It answers any recipe in the allowed ranges with a noisy measurement and can generate a history of past experiments in the 21-column mock format (observations only). In that history the supplier and the date move the recipes, so input-based warnings can be tested under a supplier or date shift (the development mocks cannot do that). The targets are the same as everywhere (dissolution at least 85, hardness 6 to 10 kP, friability at most 1.0). Why a lab and not a fixed test table: an engine picks its own experiments, so it must be able to ask for any recipe.

**What "sealed" means, precisely (the full text is `acceptance/README.md`, itself sealed):**
- **Locked, byte for byte:** `sealed_lab.py` (every formula, constant, the noise, the targets, the history generator and the 40 acceptance seeds **(version 1: 7,000,001 to 7,000,040; MOVED in version 2 on 2026-10-12 to 7,000,101 to 7,000,140, see "Sealed acceptance lab version 2" at the end of this file)**), `README.md` (this definition) and `SEAL.json` (the manifest). Line endings are normalised before fingerprinting; `.gitattributes` also pins the folder to Unix line endings.
- **Recorded:** the SHA-256 fingerprints below; the same fingerprints in `SEAL.json`; the git tag `sealed-lab-v1`; and `acceptance/LEDGER.md`, an append-only list of every creation, sealing, run and opening.
- **A violation** is any of: a change to a sealed file after sealing; any file outside `acceptance/` that imports, opens, reads, copies from or names the lab; any run that is not a designated acceptance run entered in the ledger (end of Stage 4 and in Stage 10, with the 40 sealed seeds, after the fingerprints were verified); any model, setting, threshold or engine choice made after looking at a result from it; or a ledger entry of type OPENED. The response to a violation is a new lab, never a repair.
- **What it does not do:** it is a process seal, not a technical lock. It makes tampering and outside use detectable (the fingerprints, the scan in `check_seal.bat`) and every legitimate run visible; a run outside the runner leaves no technical trace, so that rule rests on honesty and the ledger.

**Fingerprints, VERSION 1 (SUPERSEDED on 2026-10-12 by version 2, whose table is at the end of this file; SHA-256 of the bytes with CRLF turned into LF; `check_seal.bat` recomputes and compares the CURRENT ones):**

| File | SHA-256 |
|---|---|
| `acceptance/sealed_lab.py` | `957b6925310344e53305074802ae719cb0b89b59285e1c9965841c8db5d116b8` |
| `acceptance/README.md` | `ca2692210ff4bd694504c55ad214d156f29144837347c0fba69efdc153a37670` |
| `acceptance/SEAL.json` | `d6025cb360ba5258c1ad6ef1ef84a023396e817d3b0295367aedc6115c042b2c` |

**How it was made, honestly.** It was written after Stages 3a and 3b, not before modelling as planned. No Stage 3 model or result ever touched it (it did not exist), but it was written by the assistant that knew the Stage 3 findings, so its design properties were fixed in writing before the first test and are listed in the README: deterministic and independent of call order; every effect in a physically sensible direction; random recipes passing all three targets between 3% and 12% of the time; dissolution, hardness and friability each passing 15% to 60%, 20% to 60% and 55% to 95%; and, in the history, some input drifting with the date (rank correlation at least 0.25) and the supplier explaining at least 15% of some input's spread. The first test missed two (random recipes passing all three: 24.3%; date drift: 0.11), so shape constants were adjusted in two rounds, with no model or engine involved; after round two every property held (all three targets 9.0%, dissolution 22%, hardness 41.6%, friability 80%; largest date drift 0.55; supplier up to 18.5% of an input's spread). Both rounds are in the ledger. The only runs were a throwaway mechanics script before sealing; none since.

**Checks run for this step:** `check_seal.bat` only (see the next paragraph for its result). Nothing else was run: no existing code changed apart from one new line registering the check in `check_all`.

## Stage 4 (decision engine upgrade): approved plan, constants fixed in advance, and step 4-1
Plan approved 2026-10-11 as proposed, starting with 4-1, with stops after each step (4-1 the engine's brief, 4-2 a richer development lab, 4-3 the engine policy, 4-4 the development comparison, 4-5 the acceptance runner and one acceptance run). Also approved: **the one-directional import**: the engine may import the model classes from the modelling part (`cellpoc.modelling.models` only), and the modelling part may never import the engine; the guard in `checks/architecture.py` checks that direction specifically. Stage 4 does no accuracy work and no retuning: it changes only how the engine uses what Stage 3 produced.

### The permanent caveat on every acceptance report (requested 2026-10-11)
Every acceptance-run report that shows results from the sealed lab, and every other place those results appear (reports, scorecards, docs, summaries), states plainly, word for word:

> This lab's shape constants were adjusted twice by someone aware of Stage 3's findings, though no Stage 3 model or code was consulted. This is not a fully blind acceptance test.

It is written once in `cellpoc/modelling/findings.py` (`ACCEPTANCE_CAVEAT`). `check_seal.bat` fails if any file in `runs/acceptance/` (where the runner will write) lacks it, and it is tested on planted text. The acceptance runner (4-5) must put it in every report it writes.

### Stage 4 policy constants, fixed in writing BEFORE 4-3 is built (requested 2026-10-11)
None of these may be changed after any result is seen; each is either derived from statistics (no engine involved) or a judgement stated as such. They are proposed for your confirmation at the 4-1 review.

| Constant | Value | Basis |
|---|---|---|
| **Staleness watch (rule 5): window** | the last **12** trials in which that target was measured, counted per trusted target | derived below |
| **Staleness watch: threshold** | alarm when **at most 7 of the 12** measured values fall inside the 90% range the engine stated for them at decision time (the stated range times the range factor, worked out before the measurement was seen); that is a hit rate of 58% or lower. Silent until 12 such trials exist. Trials of every kind count (ordinary, exploratory, bridging) | derived below |
| **Staleness response** | that target is "stale": its probability is not used to rank recipes, and every pick is exploratory (the one-in-five cap is lifted) until at least **8 of the last 12** hit again. Other trusted targets are unaffected; if every trusted target is stale the engine only spreads recipes out | follows the approved rule 5 |
| **Exploratory-pick cap** | at most **1 trial in 5** (so at most 6 of 30) may be an exploratory pick: a candidate warned by the distance, numeric-range or unseen-category status | judgement, not measurement: keeps exploitation the majority |
| **Bridging trials** | when a context input (supplier, filler grade, instrument) takes a value never seen in the training rows, run **3** bridging trials in that context first: the 3 best measured passing recipes (by measured margin; topped up with spread-out recipes if fewer exist) | judgement: 3 is the smallest number that gives a level and a spread |
| **Range factor** | the median of the later-dates recalibration factors (each learned from earlier steps only), **never below 1**; for an even count the average of the middle two. Built in 4-1 | so no single swinging fold decides it, and a range is never narrower than stated |
| **Untrusted-target policy** | candidates are the top **20%** by chance of passing the trusted targets among candidates not warned; with no measured pass of every target yet, pick the one farthest from everything tried; once there is one, alternate that with a local search around the best measured pass using the proof of concept engine's own existing values (spread 0.07 of each range, 3 centres, candidates closer than 0.02 dropped) | reuses existing numbers; the top-20% share is a judgement |
| **Fewest rows to fit a model** | **30** usable rows (the modelling part's own minimum); before that the engine uses the proof of concept's spread-out starting recipes | existing constant |
| **Which results the engine may use** | **carried only**, and only the rule's chosen model for each. Exploratory results get no model and decide nothing; a run that uses one is labelled EXPLORATORY and excluded from acceptance and from headline numbers. Not-assessed results are only ever measured. A carried status belongs to the table it was judged on: another table needs the Stage 3 rule run again | confirmed |
| **Campaigns** | 30 trials; development comparison on 40 paired campaigns with seeds **10200 to 10239** (moved from 10000 to 10039 on 2026-10-12, with your approval, because seed 10001 had been used in early smoke runs; see the seed log below); acceptance only on the sealed seeds **7,000,101 to 7,000,140 (version 2; 7,000,001 to 7,000,040 are RETIRED, see "Sealed acceptance lab version 2")** | the proof of concept's pattern; the seed policy |

**How the watch's window and threshold were derived (statistics only; no engine was run).** The criteria, stated before the choice: if the stated range is truly honest (90% of measurements inside), a 30-trial campaign should raise a false alarm at most **5%** of the time for a target; and if the true hit rate falls to **60%** from trial 10, the watch should raise an alarm within the campaign at least **80%** of the time. Among the windows tried (10, 12, 15, 20 trials), the rule was: the smallest window meeting both, then the largest threshold. That gives 12 trials and "at most 7 hit" (false alarm 2.6%, detection 83.6%).

**Exactly what data was involved (answering the review question, 2026-10-11):** none from any trial, lab, mock, model, engine or the sealed lab. The first version of this table was not purely analytical: its first column (one look at one window) was exact binomial, but the campaign-level columns came from a simulation of 200,000 campaigns of **generated** yes/no draws at assumed hit rates of 0.9, 0.6 and 0.7. Because that counted as generated data, every figure below was then recomputed **exactly**, with no random numbers at all, by following the probability of every possible run of hits and misses through a 30-trial campaign. The exact values agree with the simulation to within 0.2 percentage points, and the table below is the exact one. So the window and threshold rest on nothing but the arithmetic of yes/no outcomes at stated hit rates.

| Window, alarm when at most k hit | False alarm when honest | Detected when the true hit rate falls to 60% | to 70% |
|---|---|---|---|
| 10, k = 5 | 1.37% | 73.74% | 42.59% |
| 10, k = 6 | 8.08% | 92.13% | 72.20% |
| **12, k = 7 (fixed)** | **2.64%** | **83.61%** | **55.61%** |
| 15, k = 9 | 1.13% | 78.24% | 46.43% |
| 15, k = 10 | 5.16% | 91.38% | 69.33% |
| 20, k = 14 | 3.12% | 89.64% | 64.05% |

**Disclosures.** (1) I first computed this table with looser criteria (false alarm at most 10%, detection at least 70%) and tightened them to 5% and 80% after seeing it once, so the choice was not made blind to the table, though the table is pure statistics and no engine result existed. Window 10 with k = 6, or window 20 with k = 14, are the nearest alternatives if you prefer an earlier or a steadier watch. (2) **This watch is built to catch a COLLAPSE, not a slow or moderate decline.** A sudden fall to a 60% hit rate is detected 83.6% of the time within a 30-trial campaign; a fall to 70% only 55.6% of the time; and a slow slide from 90% to 70% over 20 trials (then level) is detected only 50.1% of the time, and late. A mild over-confidence (a hit rate of 80%) is very unlikely to be caught in a dozen trials (not calculated; the alarm threshold is a hit rate of 58%). The watch must never be described, in any report or scorecard, as protection against gradual drift: mild over-confidence is what the range factor is for, and a gradual decline is a known gap that nothing built so far covers. (3) With up to three trusted targets each watched separately, the chance of at least one false alarm in a campaign is 7.7%.

**Confirmed by you, 2026-10-11: the 12-trial window with an alarm at 7 or fewer hits is fixed.** Also confirmed, as **provisional defaults**: the exploratory-pick cap of 1 trial in 5, the 3 bridging trials, and the top-20% untrusted-target policy. **These three may be revisited after seeing the 4-4 development-lab comparison, but never after the 4-5 sealed acceptance run**, the same discipline applied to every model setting. The window, the alarm threshold, the range factor and the other constants in the table above are not provisional. If one of the three provisional defaults is changed after 4-4, the change, the reason and the result that prompted it are written here before the 4-5 run.

**Second import approved, 2026-10-11:** the engine may also import the warning code, `cellpoc.modelling.domain`, extended the same way as the first (`cellpoc.modelling.models`): one direction only, enforced by the guard in `checks/architecture.py`, with a self-test that the reverse is still blocked, meaning the modelling part importing from the decision engine or from the briefing piece. The engine's allowed imports from the modelling part are therefore exactly `models` and `domain`; anything else in the modelling part is still refused.

### Stage 4-1: the decision engine's brief (built; stop for review)
`make_engine_brief.bat` reads the saved Stage 3 answers (nothing refitted; about 40 seconds) and writes, per table, `runs/brief/brief_<table>.json` (for the engine) and a plain-words `.md` (for you), plus `brief_summary.md`. `check_brief.bat` is the health check (30 checks; part of `check_all.bat`).

**What the brief holds, and nothing more:** for each result: its status (carried, exploratory or not assessed), the model the Stage 3 rule chose, whether its stated 90% range is honest (as stated and after recalibration), the range factor, the plain reason if it was not assessed, and which columns it is calculated from; for the table: its data-status label and any exploratory or robustness label, and the caveats (including the unifying statement and that carried status belongs to the table it was judged on). No measurement, no row of data, nothing from any lab. The shape (`cellpoc/contracts/engine_brief.py`) imports nothing from the project, is frozen after creation (the engine cannot promote an exploratory result), and a result is "usable for decisions" only when carried (derived from the status, never stored).

**Where the builder lives, and why.** In a new small bridging piece, `cellpoc/briefing/`, in the same family as `scoring` and `comparison`: it imports the modelling part and the shared shapes, and neither the modelling part nor the engine imports it. Putting it inside the modelling part would have needed a second new allowed import (modelling to the shared shapes), which you had not approved.

**The guard (approved change, built):** the engine may import from the modelling part only `cellpoc.modelling.models`; the modelling part never imports the engine, directly or through any other part; the engine still cannot reach the virtual lab or the scoring through anything it may import. Each rule has planted self-tests (an engine import of the model classes, absolute or relative, is accepted; an engine import of any other modelling module, or of the package itself, is refused; the modelling part importing the engine is refused in three wordings; an indirect route to the engine through another part is found). Nothing in the engine imports the modelling part yet. Rule 4 of the policy (warnings) will need the warning code in `cellpoc/modelling/domain.py`, which this guard does not allow: **that would be a further approved import, to be asked for at 4-3**, not assumed.

**What the briefs say on the saved answers:** 7 carried results (easy mock: dissolution, hardness, friability; hard mock: hardness, friability; real batch data: hardness and tensile strength, which are one finding), 1 exploratory (hard mock dissolution), every other result not assessed with its reason; the briefs match the 3b-1 decisions exactly and name the model the selection rule chose. **Range factors:** only 3 of the 7 carried results are widened by more than 2%: easy-mock friability (the neural-network ensemble) by 5.15, real hardness by 1.15 and real tensile strength by 1.10; the other four are at the floor of 1.00 (easy-mock dissolution 1.02). **A gap, reported and not changed:** easy-mock dissolution is over-confident both as stated and after recalibration, so its factor of 1.02 does not repair it; the staleness watch and the unvouched-candidate rules are what the engine has for that. Real hardness and tensile strength are carried but have no client-specified target, so Stage 4 does not run the engine on them.

**Checks run for this step:** the brief check (30; its first run failed once because my own expectation for an even-numbered median was wrong, the code was right), the seal check (with the new caveat tests) and the architecture guard, which runs inside every check. `check_all.bat` was run once at the end because the shared guard changed (see the result below).

### Stage 4-2: the development lab (built, 2026-10-11 to 12)
`cellpoc/virtual_lab/dev_lab.py` (the lab), `_dev_truth.py` (its hidden truth, private) and `dev_oracle.py` (scoring-only access) sit in the existing virtual-lab part; the proof of concept's own lab is untouched. The guard's hidden-truth list now covers the two new private modules, with a planted self-test. `check_devlab.bat` is the health check (26 checks; part of `check_all.bat`). `make_dev_scenarios.bat` writes the four scenario histories and runs Stage 3 on each (about 15 minutes the first time; the saved answers are reused after that).

**What it is.** The mock generator's own formula for dissolution and hardness, the 'easy' setting with no non-linear term, with the generator's own supplier and grade effects; the check compares the two on 500 random recipes and they agree to the last digit. It adds: **friability as a real constraint** (the generator's friability passes over 99% of the time and constrains nothing); a **fourth supplier, D**, which no history contains; a **history in which the supplier and the date move the recipes** (each batch draws a campaign centre, the mix drifts with the date, each centre favours a supplier and a filler grade); a **true process step** from a chosen campaign trial; and an optional **rough dissolution surface**. It answers any recipe in the allowed ranges, in a context (supplier, grade, tester, batch, time), with noisy dissolution, hardness and friability, and generates histories in the mock data's 21 columns so they go through the same ingestion and Stage 3 path as any data. It shares the context shape with the engine (`TrialContext` in the shared shapes), so the engine never imports a lab type.

**Four scenarios, fixed in advance:** *baseline* (everything predictable, no shift); *new_supplier* (history shift; the unseen supplier D takes over from campaign trial 8); *period_shift* (history shift; from trial 6 the product truly changes, dissolution down 6 and hardness up 0.8, with no sign in the inputs); *unpredictable_dissolution* (a rough dissolution surface Stage 3 should not carry). Campaign batches are 10 trials. History seeds 10101 to 10104.

**Properties written down BEFORE the lab was first tested, and the first-test results (no tuning was needed for these):**

| Property | Required | First test |
|---|---|---|
| Random recipes passing all three targets | 2% to 10% | 8.2% |
| Dissolution, hardness, friability passing individually | 15-60%, 20-60%, 55-90% | 23.3%, 58.9%, 55.1% (friability is at the lower edge) |
| Friability removing recipes that pass the other two | at least 15% | 18.1% |
| No-shift histories: date drift, supplier share of an input's spread | at most 0.10, at most 4% | 0.07, 1.4% |
| Shift histories | at least 0.25, at least 15% | 0.48, 20.0% |
| A quadratic fit on the earlier half, scored on the later half | at most 25% for the rough dissolution; at least 60% for every smooth result | 5% (then 3%); 77% to 93% |
| Supplier D shifts dissolution; the step is exactly (-6, +0.8) from trial 6 | about -6; exact | -6.0; deviation 7e-16 |

**One property FAILED on the first attempt, and was adjusted once.** The scenario "unpredictable dissolution" was designed so that Stage 3 would not carry dissolution. In the first version (3, 3.5, 2.5 and 3 cycles of a high-frequency term across the ranges) Stage 3 **did carry it, with a Gaussian process**: worst error 0.651 of the spread against the 0.70 carry line (the forest scored 0.67 to 0.74; the linear and quadratic models 0.97 to 0.99, so my own quadratic-fit test had wrongly suggested it was unlearnable). I recorded this, then changed only the frequencies to 9, 7, 8 and 7 cycles, with the success condition written down first (Stage 3 must not carry it). Second version: the best model's worst error is **0.895**, so Stage 3 does **not** assess it. That is the only adjustment; had it still been carried I would have stopped. The lab change was judged against Stage 3's own rule, not against any engine.

**What Stage 3 concludes about each scenario's history (saved in `runs/devlab/scenario_summary.md`):** baseline: dissolution (quadratic ridge, widening factor 1.00), hardness (linear ridge, 1.00) and friability (quadratic ridge, 1.00) all CARRIED. new_supplier: all three carried (dissolution 1.02, hardness 1.00, friability by the neural ensemble, factor 4.16). period_shift: all three carried (1.00 each). unpredictable_dissolution: dissolution NOT ASSESSED; hardness (linear, 1.00) and friability (neural ensemble, 4.39) carried. So Stage 3 cannot see the campaign events in advance, which is the point: the new supplier and the process step are things only the engine's rules 4 and 5 can meet.

**Honest limits.** Friability's pass share sits right at the lower edge of its band. The friability formula's constants (2.3, 0.24, 0.8, 0.3) were chosen once by me and not adjusted. Everything here is synthetic.

### Stage 4-3: the governed decision engine (built; stop for review before 4-4)
`cellpoc/decision_engine/governed.py` (the engine), `cellpoc/contracts/governed.py` (the shared shapes: trial context, target specification, observation, decision) and the brief, which now also carries the inputs each carried model was fitted on, so the engine fits exactly those. `check_engine.bat` is the health check (38 checks; part of `check_all.bat`). The engine is deliberately **not** exported from the engine package's `__init__`, so the proof of concept's import path, and its start-up, are unchanged (the PoC does not import scikit-learn).

**The five rules as actually implemented** (all constants from "Stage 4 policy constants" above):
1. **Only a carried result is modelled.** For each target the brief says carried, exploratory or not assessed. Only a carried target gets a model (the one model the Stage 3 rule chose, with its fixed settings, on exactly the inputs Stage 3 used) and a stated chance, computed from the model's 90% range as a bell-shaped spread. A target that is not carried is measured every trial and gets no model and no probability. A combined chance of passing every target is stated only when every target is modelled and none is stale.
2. **Unmodelled targets.** Candidates are the top 20% by chance of meeting the modelled targets among candidates that are not warned. With no measured recipe meeting every target, the engine picks the one farthest from everything measured; once there is one it alternates that (odd trials) with a local search (even trials) around the best measured passes, using the proof of concept engine's own spread (0.07), three centres and minimum distance (0.02).
3. **Unvouched recipes.** A candidate warned by the distance, numeric-range or unseen-category check is never an ordinary pick. It can only be an explicit exploratory pick (the warned candidate with the highest stated chance, labelled as not vouched for), at most one in any five consecutive trials, so at most six in 30. If every candidate were warned, the pick is labelled exploratory and says the cap could not be kept (it happened in none of the test runs).
4. **A new context value.** A supplier, filler grade or tester never seen in the training rows causes exactly three bridging trials, each re-measuring one of the best three distinct measured passes (the history and the engine's own earlier trials, by margin: the worst slack against any target in units of that result's own spread), in the new context. After the third, the models are refitted at once on the new data.
5. **The staleness watch.** For each modelled target, each measurement is checked against the range stated for that recipe before it was seen (the model's range widened by the brief's factor, never narrower). Seven or fewer hits in the last 12 trials make the target stale (bridging, exploratory and ordinary trials all count); it recovers when at least 8 of the last 12 hit. It catches a collapse, not a slow decline.

**Choices I had to make while building (for your review; none changes a fixed constant):**
- **A stale target is treated like an unmodelled one** (rule 2: ranked by the targets that still hold, spread out or searched locally, with the one-in-five cap lifted). My first version spread picks out blindly, ignoring the other targets, and in a 30-trial smoke run of the period-shift scenario only 12 picks were truly good; with the corrected reading 23 were. This change was made after seeing that smoke run.
- **The exploratory cap is a sliding window** (no more than one in any five consecutive trials), not a running allowance of `floor(trial / 5)`, which allowed three in a row after a stale period.
- **New cost constants, provisional like the others:** models are refitted **every 5 trials** (and at once after a new context value is bridged); 2,000 random candidates per decision (plus 1,200 local ones). Refitting every trial would make the 4-4 comparison take many hours with the neural-network and Gaussian-process models.
- The engine repeats two small helpers of the Stage 3 harness, because it may not import that module: the whole-batch inner folds that choose a ridge penalty, and the calibration group kept apart for a conformal range.

**Known-answer tests (38 checks; every one against a world where the right answer is known):**
- **An untrusted target never enters the probability:** with dissolution not assessed, no decision in the runs states a chance or a range for it, no model is fitted for it, no combined chance is stated, and every explanation says it is not modelled; replacing every dissolution value in the data with random numbers changes no prediction, range or chance of the modelled targets (3,200 candidates compared); an exploratory result is treated the same way.
- **An unvouched recipe is never an ordinary pick:** in 270 trials (3 scenarios, 3 engine seeds, 30 trials each) no exploit, spread-out or local-search pick was warned; in 14 of those trials the best-looking candidate overall WAS unvouched, so the rule really bound.
- **A leak is refused:** a measurement from the decision's own trial or any later one raises an error before anything is chosen (four ways), and corrupting trial 8's measurement changes no decision up to and including trial 8.
- Also: the watch fires at 7 or fewer hits of 12 and not at 8, slides and recovers (seven exact cases); bridging happens exactly at trials 8, 9 and 10 for the unseen supplier and again exactly three times for an unseen filler grade, with the anchors checked by an independent calculation; the stated range doubles exactly with a factor of 2 and a factor below 1 is treated as 1; with too few rows no model is fitted; with nothing carried the engine works by measurement alone; two engines given the same data decide identically; every explanation is in plain words.

**Smoke runs (one 30-trial campaign per scenario, scratch only, NOT the comparison; development seed 10901, quadratic and linear models chosen by hand):** truly good picks out of 30 (a random recipe each trial gave 1 or 2): baseline 30; new supplier 23; period shift 18; unpredictable dissolution 5. Two things to look at in 4-4: the staleness watch kept the engine in exploration for 14 and 18 of the 30 trials in the two shift scenarios (a model with no input for "which period" cannot adapt quickly to a step with only a few new rows), and it costs good picks while it explores.

**Seeds, with a disclosure.** Engine development and testing use the block 10901 to 10903. My first smoke runs, and the first version of some tests, used engine seed 10001, which lies inside the block 10000 to 10039 planned for the 4-4 comparison. That does not touch the sealed seeds, but it means a few early campaigns on one of the 4-4 seeds were seen while I fixed the stale-target reading. **Proposal for your decision: move the 4-4 campaign seeds to 10200 to 10239, a block never used in development.** Nothing is built for 4-4 yet.

**Not done, by design:** no accuracy work or retuning of any Stage 3 model (the engine refits only the model and settings Stage 3 chose); the engine is not wired into a campaign runner yet (that is 4-4); the proof of concept, its 40-campaign comparison and the dashboard are unchanged.

### Between 4-3 and 4-4: two gaps closed, what is locked, and the seed log (2026-10-12)
**1. The 270-trial unvouched-recipe test covered ONE of the three warning types, not all three.** Measured over those 270 trials (3 scenarios, 3 engine seeds, 30 trials each): the **distance** check warned 54,417 candidates, was the check that warned the best-looking candidate in 11 trials, and was the reason for all 35 exploratory picks; the **numeric-range** check warned **0** candidates and the **unseen-category** check warned **0**. Both are structural: candidates are drawn inside a design space the training data already spans, so the range check has nothing to flag, and an unseen category belongs to the whole trial's context (every candidate would be warned alike), which rule 4 (bridging) handles before any ordinary pick. (An earlier statement of "14 trials where the best-looking candidate was unvouched" used the first seeds, 10001 to 10003; with the final seeds 10901 to 10903 it is 11.) **Fix:** three new tests, one per type, each replacing the warning function with one that flags only that type over a known region (drug load above 22%), so the right answer is known: for each type, no ordinary pick lies in the region, every exploratory pick does and is labelled with only that type, and never more than one in any five consecutive trials (20 ordinary and 5 exploratory picks per type). The breakdown above is printed by the check every time.

**2. The two Stage 3 helpers repeated in the engine: kept as a copy, with a test that fails if they ever disagree.** A shared home reachable from both sides does not exist without a further guard change: the engine may import only `cellpoc.modelling.models` and `cellpoc.modelling.domain`, the modelling part may not import the briefing folder (that would be the forbidden direction), and the only way to have one copy is to rewrite Stage 3's fold code in `splits.py` and `validate.py`, which would change locked code for no change in behaviour. So the duplication stays and `check_engine.bat` now compares the engine's whole-batch inner folds and its conformal calibration group with the Stage 3 originals on every fold of 60 random tables (281 folds, 1,312 inner folds): they agree exactly, and the comparison is shown to be sensitive (a copy with 3 folds instead of 5 is caught).

**3. Two changes made DURING 4-3's build and smoke-testing, before any 4-4 campaign ran, and now LOCKED.**
- **A stale target is treated like an unmodelled one** (rule 2: ranked by the targets that still hold, spread out or searched locally, with the one-in-five cap lifted). This **completes an underspecified rule and fixes a defect in my first implementation**: the approved text says "other trusted targets are unaffected", and the first version, on a stale target, picked the recipe farthest from everything measured among those the other targets could vouch for, which discarded what those other targets already knew (their stated chances), so picks drifted into regions that failed hardness and friability. For the record, it was seen in a scratch 30-trial smoke run of the period-shift scenario on development seed 10001 (12 of 30 picks truly good before the fix, 23 after); it is a correction to what the rule means, not a search for a better number.
- **The exploratory cap is a sliding window**: no more than one exploratory pick in any five consecutive trials. This is the literal reading of "one in any five consecutive trials" (the constant "1 trial in 5, so at most 6 of 30" is unchanged); my first version used a running allowance of `floor(trial / 5)`, which allowed three exploratory picks in a row after an unused stretch, and was a bug.
- **Both readings are locked and will not change once 4-4 starts.** If a flaw appears in either during 4-4, 4-4 is stopped and reported; it is not patched in place.

**4. Provisional constants, all logged with one rule.** The exploratory-pick cap (1 in 5), the bridging count (3), the top-20% untrusted-target pool, and the two cost constants added while building, **models refitted every 5 trials** and **2,000 random candidates plus up to 1,200 local ones per decision**, are all provisional defaults: **they may be revisited after seeing the 4-4 development-lab comparison, but never after the 4-5 sealed acceptance run**. Any change after 4-4, with its reason and the result that prompted it, is written here before the 4-5 run. The staleness window and threshold (12; 7 or fewer), the range factor and the readings in item 3 are not provisional.

**5. The 4-4 campaign seeds are now 10200 to 10239** (approved), a block never used in development, and a short **seed log** is kept here: every seed used so far in Stage 4 development and smoke-testing, so it is auditable later which seeds were ever looked at before the real comparison.

| Seed(s) | Used for | What was looked at |
|---|---|---|
| engine seed **10001** | early scratch smoke runs of the engine (baseline 12 trials, new supplier 14, unpredictable dissolution 12, period shift 30 three times: the first version, after the stale-target fix, after the cap fix), and the first runs of the 270-trial test | per-trial kinds, whether each pick was truly good, chances; pass or fail |
| engine seeds **10002, 10003** | the first runs of the 270-trial test (with 10001) | pass or fail and counts only |
| engine seeds **10901, 10902, 10903** | `check_engine.bat` since the move, one 30-trial smoke campaign per scenario (10901) and the per-warning-type count | per-trial outcomes for 10901; counts for all three |
| lab and history seed **10101** | the development lab's property tests, the engine check's worlds (a 600-experiment history and the measurements), the smoke runs | pass shares, drift, noise sizes, per-trial outcomes |
| history seeds **10102, 10103, 10104** | the new-supplier, period-shift and unpredictable-dissolution scenario histories (3,000 experiments each) and Stage 3 run on them; 10102 also as the "different seed" in one determinism test | what Stage 3 concluded about each scenario |
| random-recipe draw seeds **1, 3, 5, 9** | measuring how rare a good recipe is, and the dev lab's tests | pass shares |
| random-table seeds 404 and 0 to 9 | the drift test between the engine's helpers and Stage 3's (tables of made-up data, not lab seeds) | agreement of the folds |
