**Limits.** The ungoverned engine is a proxy: it also has no warnings or exploratory picks, so it is not the governed engine with only the response switched off. It is 10 campaigns. The window also lags by design: 8 hits of the last 12 are needed, so earlier misses keep a target stale for some trials after the model has recovered. The exact answer (the governed engine with the watch still counting but the response removed) needs a new campaign-level run; it cannot be got from stored data and is not proposed.

**2. Does the watch firing lead to any correct response, or does the response work against recovery? (detection and response separated)**
- **Detection: accurate, but not specific.** In period_shift the watch fired in **every** campaign (40 of 40, 40 of 40, 80 of 80, for the current and the changed engine in both tests), at decision 13 (median; range 13 to 15), the earliest it can after a step at trial 6. The ranges did stop holding: the dissolution hit rate fell from 0.96 to about 0.2 straight after the step, for both engines. But in the **unshifted baseline** (first test, current engine, 40 campaigns) it also raised an alarm in **16 of 40 campaigns (40%)**, staying on for 5.3 trials per such campaign on average. So it detected the shift every time and also raised the alarm 40% of the time when nothing had shifted (the causes were recorded earlier: hardness under-coverage and clustered misses).
- **Did the alarm lead to a needed response? Not in this lab.** The ungoverned engine ignored it entirely and was **truly good in 100% of the 1,200 trials of the 4-4 period_shift campaigns, before and after the step**, and the chance of meeting every target it stated was about 1.00 throughout: its stated ranges were wrong by about 4.6 points on dissolution, and that never changed whether a pick met the targets (the picks sit well inside the targets). So the information "something shifted" had nothing to correct here.
- **The response worked against recovery.** Same seeds, the governed engine learned the step more slowly than the ungoverned one: dissolution hit rate 0.58 against 0.75 in trials 25 to 30, mean absolute error 3.35 against 1.90, any-target watch cleared in 2 of 10 campaigns against 7 of 10 for an exploiting engine; and it cost 9.3 to 12 recipes per campaign (the governed engine 14.7 to 16.0, the changed engine 20.5, the ungoverned engine 30.0). The record's earlier accounting puts 97% of the changed engine's remaining gap in the stale-exploration picks themselves.
- **What is not known.** Every figure above is for one mild, uniform step. The governed engine was never run on a shift that actually breaks ordinary picks (Finding A found that such shifts, to the extent they leave a winnable region, are recovered by an exploiting engine within about 10 trials: at 2.0 times the step it was 31%, 61% then 78% good in blocks of five trials). So **whether the response would help under a consequential shift is unmeasured, not shown to be bad**.

**3. Is the core problem that the watch fires correctly but reacts poorly, or that its whole design (stale means explore broadly) is the wrong response to this kind of shift?** Plainly: **on the evidence, the problem is mostly the design premise of the response, not how well it was carried out, and it is specific to this kind of shift.** The reasoning:
- The shift in this lab is a uniform offset: the dissolution error is almost entirely one-sided (mean signed error -4.55 against mean absolute error 4.55 in trials 6 to 12, and still -3.25 against 3.35 at the end). A uniform offset moves every recipe's result by the same amount, so **it spoils the model's stated ranges but not its ranking of recipes**, which is why the ungoverned engine kept picking good ones.
- The watch reads "the stated ranges stopped holding" as "the model knows nothing here" and responds as it does for an unmodelled target: it drops the model's chances altogether (no chance is used while stale) and explores. That **throws away a ranking that was still good**.
- Evidence it is the premise and not the placement: the changed engine placed those picks far better (all-stale picks 3% to 48% truly good), and still lost about half of them; those picks do not use the model's ranking, so they miss about as often as an unranked search near the old region would, while the ungoverned engine's ranked picks never missed. Better placement closed only 35 to 38% of the gap.
- **The two problems are separate and both are real:** (a) the alarm itself is accurate on a real shift but fires falsely in 40% of unshifted campaigns (a calibration matter); (b) the response (broad exploration) is mismatched to an offset-type shift. A response suited to this kind of shift would be narrow: re-centre the stated ranges on the recent errors (the offset is estimable from a few trials) and keep using the model's ranking, rather than exploring broadly. **That is an inference from this data, not a tested result.**
- **Limits of that conclusion.** The lab's step is uniform by construction. A real shift that acts differently on different recipes would break the ranking too, and then broad exploration may be the right response. The new-supplier scenario is where the engine's other response (bridging) was designed to act, and there the engine was also behind the ungoverned one (-3.8). Nothing here says the watch should be removed, and nothing is proposed.

## PROPOSAL (NOT APPROVED; nothing built, nothing run): a change to rule 5's response, and the two-scenario test that decides whether it is a general improvement (2026-10-12) [SYNTHETIC DATA]
**Status.** Written only. No code has been written or run for this proposal. It needs the project owner's approval of the whole plan below, constants and thresholds included, before anything is built. This is a bigger change than the all-stale fallback, so it gets the full proposal-then-approval treatment. **What is NOT proposed:** any change to the watch's firing rule (window of 12, alarm at 7 or fewer hits), to bridging, the exploratory cap, the warnings or the new-supplier response; any change to `governed.py` or `baselines.py`; any 4-5 work.

### 1. The recalibration response (called R)
**The idea in one sentence.** When the watch has fired for a target, do not abandon the model's ranking by default: look at the model's recent signed errors, and if they show an offset, re-centre the stated ranges by that offset and keep choosing by the (re-centred) ranking; if they show something an offset cannot fix, fall back to the engine's current response (stop using the chance, explore).
**Why the plain version is not enough (the caveat, made concrete).** A plain "re-centre on the recent signed error" fails in exactly the case the project owner flagged: if the shape of the relationship changes, an engine that keeps picking similar recipes sees errors that all share a sign, so they look like an offset, and re-centring gives it a confident, wrong ranking. Sign consistency alone cannot tell the two apart, because the picks themselves are not diverse. So R carries two checks that do not depend on the picks being diverse: a test of whether the model is still failing, and a test of whether the re-centred model still sees a promising recipe.
**Exactly what R does** (all constants fixed here, before any test; none is tuned afterwards):
1. **Unchanged:** the watch fires exactly as now (at most 7 hits of the last 12 stated ranges). R acts only on a target the watch has marked stale, and only while it is stale.
2. **Recent** means **the last 6 measurements of that target, in trial order** (including bridging trials, never a trial that has not happened yet). For each, the **signed residual** is the measurement minus the mean the CURRENT fitted model predicts for that recipe in that context (the model as refitted on everything so far, so a refit that has already absorbed part of the shift reduces the correction needed). It is the signed error, not the absolute error. A recent measurement is a **miss** if it lies outside the range the current model would state for that recipe (the model's range, widened by the brief's factor as now).
3. **Evidence classes, judged at every decision on those 6 residuals** (no estimate is used before 6 measurements of the target exist; the watch cannot fire before 12, so this is always met):
   - **FINE: at most 2 of the 6 are misses.** The model is working at the moment and the watch is only lagging (its window still holds old misses). No correction; the target is treated as modelled and chosen on as usual. (This also stops the cost of a false alarm.)
   - **OFFSET: at least 3 misses and at least 5 of the 6 residuals have the same sign.** The correction is **the median of the 6 signed residuals**; every candidate's predicted mean and stated 90% range for that target are moved by it (the width is unchanged). The chance of meeting the target is worked out from the moved values, the stated range is the moved range (so the watch counts later hits against it), and the engine keeps choosing by the chances, as in ordinary exploitation.
   - **MIXED: at least 3 misses and fewer than 5 of the same sign.** An offset cannot explain it: fall back to the engine's current response for that target (its chance is not used and the pick is exploration), exactly as now.
4. **The promise check (the second safeguard).** If, with every correction applied, the best vouched candidate's chance of meeting every modelled target together is **below 0.5**, the re-centred model no longer sees a good recipe: for that decision the stale targets fall back to the current response (explore) instead. Re-judged at every decision.
5. **The watch's clearing is untouched** (8 or more hits of the last 12 clears it); the correction simply stops being applied when it clears or when the class turns FINE.
**How it would be built, if approved (stated so the lock is not disturbed).** As a **new strategy class in a new file** (`cellpoc/decision_engine/recalibrated.py`) that inherits from the governed engine and overrides only the step where the decision is made and the prediction step; **`governed.py` and `baselines.py` are not edited**, so their recorded fingerprints stay and the lock check keeps passing. It imports nothing new from the modelling part. It inherits the seed guard. Whether R would ever replace the current engine is a separate decision after the test. Its own engine-check tests, written before the test runs: R makes the same decisions as the current engine when the watch never fires; the three classes are routed correctly on constructed residuals (including 5 of 6 against 4 of 6 same sign, 2 against 3 misses); the correction is the median of the signed residuals and moves the range without changing its width; the promise check sends a hopeless re-centred model to exploration; a future measurement is still refused. The seed-guard check gets R as a further entry point.
**Two arms are tested, so the safeguards can be judged:** **R** as above, and **N (naive)**: always re-centre by the median of the last 6 signed residuals when the watch fires and always keep the ranking, with no evidence classes and no promise check. N is a diagnostic arm, never a candidate for adoption; it shows whether the safeguards matter.

### 2. The second scenario: a shift that changes the shape (called "reshaped")
**Design.** From campaign trial 6 (the same moment as period_shift, with no sign in any input, the same history and the same brief), the true dissolution of a recipe changes by **k x 24 x (b - 0.5)**, where b is the binder share scaled to 0 to 1 over its allowed range. At k = 1 this reverses the binder's effect on dissolution about its middle value (the formula's term of -12 b becomes +12 b - 12): recipes with little binder LOSE up to 12 points, recipes with much binder GAIN up to 12 points, and recipes with a middle amount do not change. Hardness and friability are untouched. It is the opposite of a uniform offset: the change differs in size AND sign from recipe to recipe, so the ranking the model learned from the history becomes genuinely wrong, not just its stated confidence. The lab gains this as a scenario field (like the step size field added before; the four scenarios of the comparison are unchanged).
**The success condition for the scenario itself, FIXED BEFORE it is built or run**, using the **ungoverned engine only** (the governed engine and R never run on a candidate before the test; this is the discipline that exposed the dead end for the uniform step). The sizes tried are **k in {0.5, 0.75, 1.0, 1.5}**, fixed now. For each: 20 development campaigns of the ungoverned engine (seeds 11300 to 11319), 20,000 random recipes (seed 11320). A size qualifies only if ALL hold:
- **N1 (it is a reshaping, not an offset):** among the random recipes, the rank correlation (Spearman) between the true dissolution before and after the change is at most **0.6**, and at least **25%** of the recipes change by more than +3 points AND at least **25%** change by more than -3 points.
- **N2 (it hurts an engine that ignores it):** the ungoverned engine's truly-good share over trials 6 to 30 is at most half of its share over trials 1 to 5 (the same condition as before).
- **N3 (it is still winnable):** at least **0.5%** of the random recipes (100 of 20,000) are truly good after the change. (Chosen knowing the earlier lesson that the ungoverned engine finds good recipes where far fewer than 2% of random recipes qualify; it is not tuned to the result, because nothing has been measured yet.)
**The size used is the smallest k that meets N1, N2 and N3. If no size meets all three, the calibration stops and is reported, as with the uniform step: the list is not extended or changed without approval. That would be an acceptable finding, not a failure to be fixed**: it would say this lab cannot make a winnable, ranking-breaking shift that the ungoverned engine does not absorb, and R could then not be tested for safety and would not be called a general improvement.

### 3. The full test plan, FIXED BEFORE ANYTHING IS RUN
**Seeds, reserved now, never used before and separate from everything in the seed log:** **11300 to 11339** (11300 to 11319 and 11320 for calibrating the reshaped scenario; 11321 to 11339 spare) and **11400 to 11479** (the test). None overlaps 10101 to 10104, 10200 to 10239, 10300 to 10339, 10901 to 10970, 11000 to 11039 or 11100 to 11179.
**What runs (after the engine class, its checks, the scenario and the tool are built and the scenario size is fixed):**
- **period_shift** (the original, mild uniform step) and **reshaped** (the size chosen above): **80 paired campaigns each** (seeds 11400 to 11479), four arms on the identical lab day: **G0** (the current engine), **R**, **N** and the **ungoverned** engine (reference).
- **baseline, new_supplier and unpredictable_dissolution** (exactly as in 4-4): **40 paired campaigns each** (seeds 11400 to 11439), arms **G0 and R** only (this is the no-harm check).
- About 880 campaigns, roughly 90 to 120 minutes of computing, saved as they finish. Scored and compared with the 4-4 measures and the same paired bootstrap 95% interval (2,000 resamples, the 4-4 bootstrap seed). The run records, per trial, each target's stated range, the measurement, the pick kind and which evidence class applied, so the honesty condition below can be read from it.
**Predictions, written before the result (so they cannot be adjusted afterwards):**
- *period_shift:* R should recover most of the gap: about **27 to 30** truly good recipes per campaign against about 15 for G0 and 30 for the ungoverned engine (R minus G0 about +11 to +15). N should do about as well as R.
- *reshaped:* **N should hurt** (below G0, by several recipes), because the recent errors of its clustered picks all share a sign, so it re-centres and keeps a ranking that is now wrong. **R should be close to G0** (within about 2 recipes), because its MIXED class or its promise check should route the decisions to exploration. I am not sure the safeguards fire as intended; that uncertainty is what the test is for. I do not know in advance whether G0 beats the ungoverned engine in this scenario; that is a reported outcome.
**The result decides, by these conditions fixed in advance, all judged by the bootstrap 95% interval and never by the point estimate alone:**
- **P1 (it helps on the shift it was built for):** period_shift, R minus G0: the interval's **lower end is at least +7** truly good recipes per campaign (at least about half of the roughly 15-recipe gap to the ungoverned engine, with the interval not allowed below that; the all-stale fallback closed 35 to 38% and was not enough).
- **P2 (it is safe where the ranking really is wrong):** reshaped, R minus G0: the interval's **lower end is at least -3** (a loss of more than 3 recipes per campaign cannot be ruled out otherwise).
- **P3 (it does no harm elsewhere):** in each of baseline, new_supplier and unpredictable_dissolution, R minus G0 is not "worse" by the 4-4 verdict rule (the interval is not wholly below 0). The lower end is reported too.
- **P4 (its stated ranges and chances stay honest while it is correcting):** in period_shift, over the trials in which R re-centred a range, the share of measurements inside the re-centred stated range is at least **0.75**, and, among R's decisions stating a chance of meeting every target of at least 0.8, at least **90%** were truly good.
**What the outcomes mean:**
- **"A genuine improvement to rule 5": P1, P2, P3 and P4 all met.** Only then is R a candidate to replace the current response (still a separate approval, with the usual engine-lock procedure).
- **"Works for one kind of shift and is not a safe general fix": P1 met but P2 not met** (R cannot be shown to be safe where the ranking changes shape). R is then not adopted as a general rule; whether a detector for the two kinds of shift is worth designing is a separate proposal.
- **"Does not help": P1 not met.** Not adopted, whatever P2 says.
- **P1 and P2 met but P3 or P4 not met:** not adopted; the reason is recorded.
- **N is reported with no pass mark.** If N hurts in reshaped and R does not, the safeguards earned their place. If N does not hurt, the safeguards are unneeded complexity and that is said plainly.
- **One test, no second attempt.** The bars are not changed after the data is in, the result is not pooled with anything, and a miss ends this proposal; any further idea comes as a new proposal on new seeds. Whichever way it goes, every result is reported in full, including the ones that do not look good.
**Honest limits, stated now.** (1) The uniform and the reshaped scenarios are two synthetic examples from one smooth formula family; passing both would not prove R general, and a real client's shifts could be neither. (2) The reshaped scenario might not be buildable (see N1 to N3); that is an outcome, not a defect. (3) The residuals are measured against a model that has been refitted on those same measurements, so they run slightly small; with about 3,000 history rows behind each model the effect should be small, but it is not zero. (4) R adds four constants (6, 2, 5 and 0.5); they are fixed here and not tuned. (5) The false-alarm problem (40% of unshifted campaigns) is a calibration matter this proposal does not address, though the FINE class should blunt its cost; the baseline arm will show whether it does.
**Seed log, reserved now:** 11300 to 11339 and 11400 to 11479 (see above). Nothing else is used.
**Awaiting the project owner's decision:** approve the plan as written, or amend it. Nothing is built before that.

### The rule-5 recalibration proposal: APPROVED as written, with one clarification and one addition (2026-10-12)
**Approved by the project owner:** the plan above as written (R, N, the reshaped scenario, the test plan, P1 to P4, the decision table, the seeds 11300 to 11339 and 11400 to 11479), to be built and run once, with the bars fixed and every result reported in full. Still no 4-5 and no Stage 5 to 7. The clarification and the addition below are part of the record from now on and were written BEFORE anything was built or run.

**Clarification: what R does in the early window after the watch fires, and whether there is a gap.**
- **There is no gap, and R does not fall back to FINE or MIXED by default.** The watch cannot fire until a target has 12 checked measurements, so on the first decision at which it is stale (decision 13 at the earliest, which is where it fired in 240 of 240 period_shift campaigns), 12 measurements of the target already exist and the last 6 are available. R classifies from those 6 at that very decision: FINE, OFFSET or MIXED is decided by the evidence, never assumed. R acts from decision 13, the decision at which the current engine begins to lose recipes (its first not-good picks after the alarm were at 13: 0.00 not-good trials in 1 to 5, 0.17 in 6 to 12, then about 3 per block of six from 13 on).
- **What R does NOT do is act before the watch fires.** In trials 6 to 12 (after the step, before the alarm) R is exactly the current engine; that window costs little in the stored data (the current engine's not-good trials there were 0.17 per campaign, all of them exploratory picks of rule 3, none of them ordinary picks), and R does not try to change it.
- **The 6 measurements are not necessarily all "fresh" (after the change).** The engine does not know when a change began. At decision 13 in period_shift they are trials 7 to 12, all after the step at trial 6. If a change began later, so that the last 6 mix old and new measurements, the watch would not yet have fired on that evidence (it needs at least 5 misses in the last 12), and the median of the 6 residuals and the "5 of 6 the same sign" rule tolerate one stray old measurement; with two or more strays the evidence is likely to come out MIXED or FINE, which is the honest answer for a mixed picture. In the first decisions after firing, the correction is therefore an estimate from the 6 most recent trials, recomputed at every later decision, and it is expected to be rougher then than later.
- **What the test shows about it.** The class chosen at every decision is recorded, so the report states how many first stale decisions were classed OFFSET, FINE or MIXED, and the not-good trials of R by block of trials after firing (13 to 18, 19 to 24, 25 to 30), against the current engine's, so it is visible whether R helps right after trial 13 or only once more data has accumulated.

**Addition to the calibration of the reshaped scenario: the watch must be shown to fire (N4).** A fourth condition is added to the three in the plan, fixed now:
- **N4 (the shift is detectable by the watch):** applying the watch's own rule (at most 7 hits of the last 12 stated ranges, checked at decisions 13 to 30) to the ungoverned engine's own recorded stated ranges and measurements in the 20 calibration campaigns, the rule fires for at least one target in **at least 19 of the 20 campaigns** (95%). It is also reported per target, with the median decision of the first firing. The governed engine is NOT run on a candidate size before the test (the ungoverned engine's hit sequences stand in for it, as in the earlier analysis; they are a proxy, since the governed engine's picks differ).
- **The size used is now the smallest k meeting N1, N2, N3 AND N4.** If none does, the calibration stops and is reported, as before.
- **In the test itself, the watch's firing is reported for every governed arm** (campaigns in which it fired at least once, and the first decision), in period_shift and in reshaped, next to the results. **Pre-stated:** it should fire in at least 95% of the reshaped campaigns of the current engine (G0). **If it does not, that is reported prominently, so that a P1 or P2 result can never be explained as "the watch never detected it" or silently excused by it: P1 and P2 are still computed exactly as fixed.**

### The recalibration test: built, and the reshaped scenario's calibration ended at its pre-registered stop rule: NO size qualifies, so the test has NOT run (2026-10-12) [SYNTHETIC DATA]
**What was built (all checked, none of it touches the locked engine files).**
- `cellpoc/decision_engine/recalibrated.py`: the recalibrated response (R) and its naive variant (N), as a new class that inherits from the governed engine; `governed.py` and `baselines.py` are not edited, so their fingerprints (`afdc72475aad...`, `66b0c3190...`) and the lock check are unchanged. It inherits the seed guard. Constants as fixed: 6, 2, 5 and 0.5.
- `checks/recalibrated_check.py` (`check_recalibrated.bat`, 19 checks, in `check_all`): the evidence classes are exact (8 constructed cases, including signed against absolute error and fewer than 6 measurements); R makes exactly the same decisions as the current engine until the watch fires; **no gap** (at the first stale decision every stale target is already classed from its last 6 measurements); a correction moves the range by exactly that amount, keeps the width and recomputes the chance; the promise check, FINE and MIXED route as written; N never falls back; the future and sealed seeds are still refused; nothing written. One of my own test expectations had to change on first run: in the check's small 600-row world a refit absorbs a few shifted rows at once, so FINE is the right class there; the "no gap" test now uses the real 3,000-row history (the behaviour of the engine was not changed). On a development campaign in the real configuration (seed 10930, already used) R classed all three targets OFFSET at decision 13 with corrections of -4.95, +1.0 and -0.23 against the true step of -6, +0.8 and -0.19 (an implementation sanity check, not a result).
- The lab: a `reshaped(k)` scenario (the binder's effect on dissolution reverses: k x 24 x (binder share - 0.5), from trial 6), its oracle statistics, and one new `check_devlab.bat` check (28 checks, it had 27); the four comparison scenarios are unchanged. The seed-guard check now also covers R (10 checks).
- `tools/stage4_recalibration_test.py`: calibrate, run and report, with P1 to P4 exactly as fixed.

**The calibration of the reshaped scenario (the ungoverned engine only, 20 campaigns per size on seeds 11300 to 11319, 20,000 random recipes on seed 11320), as fixed:**

| Size k | N1: rank correlation (at most 0.6); gain over 3 points / lose over 3 points (each at least 25%) | N2: ungoverned truly good, trials 1 to 5 -> 6 to 30, ratio (at most 0.50) | N3: random recipes truly good after (at least 0.5%) | N4: the watch's rule fires, of 20 (at least 19); median first decision | Meets all four |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 0.50 | 0.94; 25% / 24% -> **no** | 100% -> 100%, 1.00 -> **no** | 5.89% yes | 9 -> **no** (14) | no |
| 0.75 | 0.86; 34% / 33% -> **no** | 100% -> 100%, 1.00 -> **no** | 6.80% yes | 16 -> **no** (13) | no |
| 1.00 | 0.75; 38% / 37% -> **no** | 100% -> 100%, 1.00 -> **no** | 7.89% yes | 19 yes (13) | no |
| 1.50 | 0.49; 42% / 42% yes | 100% -> 90%, 0.90 -> **no** | 10.08% yes | 20 yes (13) | **no** |

**By the rule fixed in advance: "if no size meets all of them, the calibration stops and is reported; the list is not extended or changed without approval."** No size was chosen. The list is not extended.
**What the numbers say.**
- **The watch detects the reshaping reliably at the sizes that reshape** (N4 met at 1.0 and 1.5; at 1.5 it fires in 20 of 20 campaigns on dissolution, at decision 13). So a later result could not be explained as "the watch never saw it".
- **But the shift does not hurt the engine that ignores it.** The ungoverned engine's truly-good share is 100% in every block at 0.5, 0.75 and 1.0, and at 1.5 it dips only to 87%, 86%, 88%, then 92% and 98% in blocks of five trials from trial 6. Only at 1.5 is the shift a genuine reshaping by N1, and there the ungoverned engine loses 10%, not the 50% N2 asks for.
- **Why, as far as the data allows an explanation (an inference, not tested).** The reversal pivots about the middle of the binder range, so recipes with a middle amount of binder do not change at all. The ungoverned engine's good recipes appear to sit where the change is small, and the lab's good region is wide enough for it to find new good recipes within a few trials after the change (as it did after the uniform steps). I did not see this before measuring it; a different pivot or a different ingredient might have hit the engine's operating region, but choosing that is a new design, not an extension of this grid.
- **This is the same kind of dead end as Finding A:** in this lab, an engine that simply keeps refitting and searching locally absorbs shifts that leave a reachable good region, whether they are offsets or reshapings of this kind. It is an acceptable finding, as the proposal said.
**Consequences, by the proposal's own wording.** The reshaped scenario cannot be built at a qualifying size, so R **cannot be tested for safety where the ranking is wrong** (P2 cannot be evaluated) and **cannot be called a general improvement**. The test has NOT run: no campaign has been run on seeds 11400 to 11479, which stay reserved and unused; seeds 11300 to 11320 are spent on this calibration. Nothing is adopted, and the engine and rule 5 are unchanged. **What is not decided:** whether to stop here, to run the part of the test that does not need the reshaped scenario (period_shift for P1 and P4, and the three no-harm scenarios for P3, which could never by itself make R a general improvement), or to design a different reshaped scenario as a new proposal on new seeds. That is for the project owner.

### Decision: run only the part of the recalibration test that does not need the reshaped scenario; the reshaped scenario is closed (2026-10-12)
**Decided by the project owner.** Option 3. **No redesigned reshaped scenario is attempted, now or as a follow-up.** The two-scenario test of the proposal is therefore not run; what is run, once, is the part that does not need the reshaped scenario, exactly as already planned for those parts:
- **period_shift (the original, mild uniform step): 80 paired campaigns, arms G0 (the current engine), R and the ungoverned engine** (N is not run: it was a diagnostic arm for the reshaped scenario), for **P1 and P4**.
- **baseline, new_supplier and unpredictable_dissolution: 40 paired campaigns each, arms G0 and R**, for **P3**.
- **Seeds 11400 to 11479** (11400 to 11439 for the three no-harm scenarios), as reserved. About 440 campaigns. **P2 is not evaluated** (it needs the reshaped scenario).
- **The bars are exactly as already written, none changed:** P1 (period_shift, R minus G0: the 95% interval's lower end at least +7 truly good recipes per campaign); P3 (in each of the three scenarios, R minus G0 not "worse" by the 4-4 verdict rule); P4 (in period_shift, over the trials in which R re-centred a range, at least 75% of measurements inside the re-centred stated range, and at least 90% of the decisions stating a chance of at least 0.8 truly good). Same paired bootstrap (2,000 resamples, the 4-4 bootstrap seed). One attempt; every result reported in full whichever way it goes.
**The verdict, fixed before the run, in the project owner's words.**
- **If P1, P3 and P4 all hold:** "R demonstrably fixes the specific problem it was designed for (offset shifts breaking calibration while ranking stays valid), with no harm shown in the other three scenarios. It is NOT shown to be a general fix for rule 5, since no reshaping (ranking-breaking) scenario has been found that both breaks the ungoverned engine and remains winnable." (The report will add the limits of the evidence: one mild uniform step in one synthetic lab, one size of it.)
- **If P1 or P3 fails, R is not adopted in any form, full stop.** (P4 failing alone also means R is not adopted, as the plan's table says.)
- **Adoption of R, even on a pass, is a separate approval:** the engine-lock procedure (the fingerprint entry, the engine-check tests) and a decision on how R would sit in the platform engine. Nothing is adopted by the test itself.
**Seed log.** 11400 to 11479: to be spent on this partial test; not to be used to judge any later engine change. 11300 to 11320: spent (the calibration). 

### Named observation (separate from R's results): the engine's search absorbs the shifts tried in this lab (2026-10-12)
**What was seen, twice, by two independent designs.**
1. **Uniform step (Finding A):** at 1.5, 2.0, 2.5, 3.0 and 4.0 times the original step, the ungoverned engine either lost little (1.5 times: 96.6% truly good over trials 6 to 30; 2.0 times: 63.2% overall but 31% in trials 6 to 10 and 78% by trials 26 to 30) or lost almost everything only when almost no good recipe was left (2.5 times and above, where 0.16% or fewer of random recipes were still good).
2. **Reshaping (the binder's effect reversed):** at 0.5, 0.75 and 1.0 the ungoverned engine's truly-good share was **100% in every block**; at 1.5, the only size that reshapes the ranking by the pre-set standard (rank correlation 0.49), it dipped to 87% and was back to 98% by the last block.
**The observation.** In this lab, an engine that simply keeps refitting its models and searching around its best measured recipes absorbs the shifts that were tried, within a few trials, as long as a reachable good region remains. The two designs differ (an offset that spoils the stated ranges, a reversal that changes the ranking) and ran into the same wall.
**What it is probably due to (an inference from the engine's design and these results; not tested).** The engine's picks concentrate in a small region of the design space (the best recipes by margin, with local search around the best measured passes). A shift that moves other regions much more than that one is invisible to its results, and a shift big enough to move that region either leaves other good recipes within reach, which the search finds within a few trials, or leaves none. The lab's good region is also wide in some directions, so there is room to move.
**What it means.** (a) It is a statement about this engine's search mechanics and this lab, and a real limit on what this lab can test: **it cannot tell the engine's responses to a shift apart on a shift that matters to ordinary picks**, because ordinary picks are rarely hurt by the shifts it can produce. (b) Claims that the governance (the watch, bridging) protects against such shifts are therefore **untested here, not refuted**: the governed engine was never run on a shift that hurt the ungoverned engine, because none was found that was also winnable. (c) The lab is one smooth formula family, and a real process could differ.
**Closed:** no further attempt at a reshaped or harsher scenario is made in this lab.

### The partial recalibration test: result (2026-10-12) [SYNTHETIC DATA]. P1, P3 and P4 are all MET; P2 was not evaluated. R is NOT adopted by this test (adoption is a separate approval).
**Run.** 480 campaigns (the plan said "about 440"; it is 240 for period_shift plus 240 for the three no-harm scenarios), seeds 11400 to 11479 (11400 to 11439 for the three), run once, exactly as fixed. The tool's report had one bug on its first run (it looked for the watch flag in the wrong place, in the "reported without a pass mark" section only); fixed, and the report rerun on the same stored campaigns. P1, P3 and P4 are computed by separate code and were never affected. Raw output: `runs/followup/recal/` (not in git).

**Truly good recipes per 30-trial campaign (min to max), paired by lab day.**

| Scenario | G0 (current) | R | Ungoverned | R minus G0 (95% interval; wins/ties/losses) | Verdict |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| period_shift (80 campaigns) | 16.3 (11 to 30) | 28.5 (26 to 30) | 30.0 (30 to 30) | **+12.22 (+11.26 to +13.16; 78/2/0)** | **better** |
| baseline (40) | 29.6 (27 to 30) | 29.7 (28 to 30) | not run | +0.12 (-0.10 to +0.40; 5/31/4) | no clear difference |
| new_supplier (40) | 25.6 (18 to 30) | 28.5 (27 to 30) | not run | **+2.92 (+1.90 to +4.00; 21/19/0)** | **better** |
| unpredictable_dissolution (40) | 5.0 (1 to 10) | 5.2 (1 to 10) | not run | +0.15 (-0.10 to +0.42; 7/28/5) | no clear difference |

**The conditions, exactly as fixed in advance.**
- **P1 (period_shift, R minus G0: the interval's lower end at least +7): MET.** +12.22, interval +11.26 to +13.16.
- **P3 (R minus G0 not "worse" in each of the three): MET in all three.** baseline +0.12 (-0.10 to +0.40); new_supplier +2.92 (+1.90 to +4.00); unpredictable_dissolution +0.15 (-0.10 to +0.42). **The stricter reading of "no harm" (the interval's lower end at or above 0) is met in new_supplier only; in baseline and unpredictable_dissolution the lower end is -0.10, with a positive point estimate and an interval that includes 0.** "No harm shown" is true; "shown not to harm at all" is not claimed.
- **P4 (honest ranges and chances while re-centring): MET.** 95.8% of 1,489 re-centred checks fell inside the re-centred stated range (bar 75%); 98.0% of 2,283 decisions stating a chance of at least 0.8 were truly good (bar 90%).
- **P2: not evaluated** (it needs the reshaped scenario, which was not built).

**The verdict, by the wording fixed in advance:** *R demonstrably fixes the specific problem it was designed for (offset shifts breaking calibration while ranking stays valid), with no harm shown in the other three scenarios. It is NOT shown to be a general fix for rule 5, since no reshaping (ranking-breaking) scenario has been found that both breaks the ungoverned engine and remains winnable.* Limits, stated with it: one mild uniform step, one size of it, in one synthetic lab; adoption of R is a separate approval (the engine-lock procedure and a decision on how it would sit in the platform engine). **R is not adopted by this test.**

**Reported without a pass mark, including what does not look clean.**
- **R helps right after the watch fires, not only later (the early-window question).** Not-good trials per campaign in period_shift: trials 6 to 12 (before the watch can fire): G0 0.23, R 0.23, ungoverned 0.00 (the same exploratory picks of rule 3); trials 13 to 18: **G0 4.41, R 0.12**; 19 to 24: G0 4.56, R 0.26; 25 to 30: G0 4.49, R **0.85**. At the first stale decision R classed 151 stale targets OFFSET and 17 FINE (none MIXED), so it acted from decision 13.
- **R's not-good trials rise in the last block (0.12, 0.26, then 0.85).** Not investigated; no explanation is claimed. It is small against G0's 4.5 but it is the reason R is still behind the ungoverned engine.
- **R does not match the ungoverned engine:** R minus ungoverned -1.46 (-1.70 to -1.23). R closes **89%** of the 13.7-recipe gap between G0 and the ungoverned engine in period_shift; the watch's response is now a small cost instead of the dominant one.
- **The watch itself is unchanged and still fires as before:** in 80 of 80 period_shift campaigns for G0 and R (first decision 13, range 13 to 14); in the unshifted baseline R's run saw it fire in 20 of 40 campaigns (as the false-alarm record says); in new_supplier 28 of 40; in unpredictable_dissolution 16 of 40. In the three no-harm scenarios R used the offset correction for a share of the stale targets at stale decisions (counted per stale target per decision: baseline 67 of 136, new_supplier 68 of 219, unpredictable_dissolution 43 of 155) and FINE for most of the rest; MIXED was rare (2 in period_shift, 4, 1 and 16 in the others). **The promise check never sent a decision to exploration in any scenario**, so it was not exercised by this test.
- **R improved new_supplier (+2.92, G0 25.6, R 28.5), which was not a prediction or a design aim.** Not investigated; it suggests that some of the current response's cost also falls on the new-supplier scenario's later trials.
- **What this test does not separate.** The ungoverned engine reaches 30.0 in period_shift by simply ignoring the alarm. R reaches 28.5 by correcting the ranges and keeping the ranking. The test shows R removes the cost of the watch's response and keeps its stated ranges and chances honest (P4); it does **not** show that re-centring does anything better than not reacting at all, because the naive arm and a "do nothing" arm were not part of this partial test (N was only a diagnostic for the reshaped scenario). The value of R over ignoring the alarm is the honesty of the ranges and chances, not recipes.
**Seed log.** 11400 to 11479: spent on this test; not to be used to judge any later engine change. **Nothing else is run or proposed.** The reshaped scenario stays closed; the engine files, rule 5 and the platform engine are unchanged.

## Adoption of R (2026-10-12): the recalibrated response is now the platform engine's rule 5 response
**Approved by the project owner**, under this exact scope, to be carried word for word into any future summary, report or scorecard that describes the engine's rule 5:

> R is adopted for its demonstrated purpose: keeping stated ranges and chances honest while reacting to a detected shift, confirmed on an offset-style shift (period_shift) with a +11 to +13 recipe-per-campaign improvement over the prior response, no clearly demonstrated harm in the other three tested scenarios (though two of those three show intervals including zero, not a clean positive no-harm result), and no evidence either way on ranking-breaking (reshaping) shifts, since no scenario of that kind has been found that is both genuinely ranking-breaking and winnable. R is not shown to outperform simply ignoring a false alarm; it is shown to react honestly where the current response reacts dishonestly.

(The same sentence is the constant `ADOPTED_SCOPE` in `cellpoc/decision_engine/recalibrated.py`, and `check_recalibrated.bat` fails if the two ever differ.)
**What the adoption is, in the code.**
- `RecalibratedStrategy` (R, with `naive=False`) in `cellpoc/decision_engine/recalibrated.py` is the platform engine. Its rule 5 response replaces the earlier one (drop the chance of a stale target and explore) wherever the evidence shows an offset (class OFFSET) or a model that is still working (class FINE); where the evidence is mixed (class MIXED) or the corrected model sees no promising recipe, the earlier response is still used, as designed and as tested. The naive variant (N) stays in the file as a diagnostic arm and is never to be used as an engine.
- `governed.py` and `baselines.py` are **not edited**; `governed.py` keeps its own rule 5 text, which describes the response R replaces, and remains the reference engine the 4-4 results were recorded with (`build_strategy("governed")` in the development comparison; the adopted engine is `build_strategy("recalibrated")`, which is not one of the three strategies of the four-scenario 4-4 comparison, so that comparison and its stored results are unchanged).
- Everything else in the engine is as it was: rules 1 to 4, the watch's firing and clearing rule (window of 12, alarm at 7 or fewer hits), bridging, the exploratory cap, the warnings, the seed guard.
- **Engine lock.** `cellpoc/decision_engine/recalibrated.py` is added to the locked engine files, as the earlier adopted changes were. Fingerprints (SHA-256 of the bytes with CRLF turned into LF; the lock check in `check_comparison.bat` reads the latest entry recorded for each file and fails if a file differs):

| File | SHA-256 |
|---|---|
| `cellpoc/decision_engine/governed.py` | `afdc72475aad05ff74003d395b9539675ec8510d2a3056680ec172d914e30b83` |
| `cellpoc/decision_engine/baselines.py` | `66b0c31908e37e4af3a80fcd4f97c29cf7c99acd353d94920a90d93821bac877` |
| `cellpoc/decision_engine/recalibrated.py` | `dff382fd8deb8c420316c7e8b71774b2eb714bd6c7f01adb0f306d616f3f6e50` |

(The first two are unchanged from the entries after the seed guard; this is the first entry for `recalibrated.py`, which was not locked while it was a test arm.)
**What the evidence was** (recorded above, "The partial recalibration test: result"): period_shift R minus the current engine +12.22 (+11.26 to +13.16; 78 wins, 2 ties, 0 losses); baseline +0.12 (-0.10 to +0.40) and unpredictable_dissolution +0.15 (-0.10 to +0.42), both with an interval that includes 0; new_supplier +2.92 (+1.90 to +4.00); stated ranges 95.8% inside and stated chances of 0.8 or more 98.0% truly good while re-centring. P2 (the reshaped scenario) was never evaluated.
**Two things adopted but UNEXPLAINED, to be watched, not investigated now.** If either reappears in the 4-5 acceptance run or in any later development comparison, it is to be flagged as a specific thing to look at then.
1. **The rising not-good trials in R's last block:** in period_shift, R's not-good trials per campaign were 0.12 (trials 13 to 18), 0.26 (19 to 24) and **0.85 (25 to 30)**. It is small against the earlier engine's 4.4 to 4.6 per block, but it is why R is still 1.46 recipes behind the ungoverned engine, and nobody knows why it rises. No explanation is claimed and none was investigated.
2. **The new_supplier improvement:** R minus the earlier engine in new_supplier was **+2.92** (+1.90 to +4.00); it was not predicted and not designed for. No explanation is claimed and none was investigated.
**Seed log.** Nothing new is used by the adoption.

## Stage 4 as it now stands: the summary (2026-10-12) [SYNTHETIC DATA]
**Status in one line.** Steps 4-1 to 4-4 are built and recorded; the engine's rule 5 response has been replaced by R (adopted under the scope above); the sealed acceptance lab (version 2) exists and is untouched; **4-5 is not started and stays gated.** Everything here was measured in the synthetic development lab; nothing says anything about real tablets. Last full `check_all` (at the adoption): 947 passed, 0 failed, 1 old robot-reach warning (all 20 checks, 16 min 56 s).

### What is built
- **4-1, the engine's brief:** what Stage 3 concluded, in the form the engine reads (a result is carried, exploratory or not assessed; only a carried result may be modelled; the range factor, never below 1).
- **4-2, the development lab:** the mock generator's formula plus friability, an unseen supplier D and a true process step; four scenarios (baseline, new_supplier, period_shift, unpredictable_dissolution); scoring on the lab's truth in each trial's own context.
- **4-3, the governed engine and its five rules:** (1) only a carried result is modelled; (2) an unmodelled target is handled by alternating the farthest recipe with a local search, among the top 20% by chance; (3) a recipe no per-recipe check can vouch for is only ever an exploratory pick, at most one in five trials; (4) a new context value (supplier, grade) causes 3 bridging trials; (5) the staleness watch (12 checks, alarm at 7 or fewer hits).
- **4-4, the development comparison:** 480 campaigns (seeds 10200 to 10239). The engine as it was then did **worse than the same models without governance in all four scenarios** (-0.5, -3.8, -14.0 and -3.5 truly good recipes per 30-trial campaign) and **better than random recipes in all four**.
- **The sealed acceptance lab, version 2** (never run; caveat permanent; seed blocks 7,000,101 to 7,000,140 live, 7,000,001 to 7,000,040 retired) and **the seed guard** (the code itself refuses a sealed seed, in the development lab, the comparison and every engine class, with its own check).
- **The engine lock:** `governed.py`, `baselines.py` and now `recalibrated.py` are covered by the fingerprint check in `check_comparison.bat`.

### What is ADOPTED
- **R, the recalibration response in rule 5** (`recalibrated.py`), under the scope above: when the watch fires, the last 6 measurements decide: FINE (the model is working), OFFSET (re-centre the stated range and keep choosing by the ranking) or MIXED (keep the old response); a promise check sends a hopeless corrected model back to the old response. On the offset-style shift (period_shift) it raised truly good recipes from 16.3 to 28.5 per campaign (the ungoverned engine: 30.0), its stated ranges stayed honest (95.8% inside) and its stated chances stayed honest (98.0%); no clearly demonstrated harm elsewhere; **not shown to beat ignoring a false alarm; no evidence either way on ranking-breaking shifts.**
- **The seed guard on the engine classes** (adopted earlier, after the 4-4 review).

### What was TRIED and REJECTED (each recorded in full above)
1. **A harsher period shift (Finding A):** every size either left the ungoverned engine unhurt or left no winnable region; closed as a finding about the lab, not pursued.
2. **The all-stale fallback fix** (when every target is stale, alternate the farthest candidate with a local search instead of always the farthest): tested twice on fresh seeds (+5.78, interval +4.92 to +6.60; then +5.11, interval +4.42 to +5.80) against a bar of an interval lower end of at least +5; **missed the bar both times, not adopted, reverted**. (It never lost a campaign; R, which changes the response itself rather than where it explores, gained more than twice as much.)
3. **The reshaped scenario** (the binder's effect reversed, to test a ranking-breaking shift): calibrated on the ungoverned engine; no size met all four pre-registered conditions (at the only size that reshapes the ranking the ungoverned engine lost 10%, not half); **closed, no redesign**.
(A heavier alternative, weighting the farthest-candidate choice by closeness to the old good recipes, was written as "not proposed" and never tested. There was no separate "full exploration redesign".)

### What is still OPEN
1. **The watch's false-alarm rate (about 40% of unshifted campaigns raise an alarm; 20 of 40 in the latest test).** A calibration matter, not an arithmetic one: the stated 90% ranges are hit only about 82% of the time for hardness, and misses cluster instead of arriving independently, which the plan's 7.7% assumed. R softens what a false alarm costs (the baseline difference was +0.12) but does not change how often the watch fires.
2. **The unexplained rise in R's not-good trials in the last block (0.12, 0.26, 0.85)**: adopted but unexplained; to be watched if it reappears.
3. **The unexplained new_supplier gain (+2.92)**: adopted but unexplained; to be watched.
4. **The reshaping question:** R has never faced a shift that changes the ranking, and the lab cannot produce one that is both ranking-breaking and winnable (the named observation: the engine's search absorbs the shifts tried). So whether R is safe there is unknown, and the promise check, which exists for that case, was never triggered in any test.
5. **R is not shown to beat doing nothing about a false alarm:** the ungoverned engine reaches 30.0 in period_shift by ignoring the alarm; R reaches 28.5 and is honest about its ranges. The value claimed is honesty, not recipes.
6. **The remaining cost of governance elsewhere** (indicative only: the ungoverned engine was not run on the final seeds for these three, so the figures use the first all-stale test's seeds and are not a paired comparison): baseline about -0.3, new_supplier about -1.5, **unpredictable_dissolution about -4.4** (the adopted engine's 5.2 against the ungoverned engine's 9.6; the unmodelled-target handling of rules 1 and 2 is what governs there, and R does not touch it).
7. **The adopted engine has not been through the formal four-scenario comparison** (the 4-4 result describes the earlier engine). What exists for it is the partial test (period_shift 80 campaigns; three scenarios 40 each).

### Seed ledger (nothing in a spent block may be used to judge a later engine change)
**Spent:** 10200 to 10239 (4-4; period_shift governed replayed once to recover recipes); 10930 to 10970 (Finding B and the step-size calibration); 11000 to 11039 and 11100 to 11179 (the all-stale tests); 11300 to 11320 (the reshaped calibration); 11400 to 11479 (the partial recalibration test). **Reserved, unused:** 10300 to 10339 (the period_shift v2 comparison, now moot since Finding A is closed) and 11321 to 11339 (spare). **Sealed:** 7,000,101 to 7,000,140 live, 7,000,001 to 7,000,040 retired.

## 4-4R: the development comparison for the adopted (recalibrated) engine: design FIXED BEFORE ANY RUN (2026-10-12)
**Why.** The adopted engine (`RecalibratedStrategy`, R) had a full matched-seed three-arm comparison on only one of the four scenarios (period_shift). For baseline and unpredictable_dissolution there was no ungoverned arm on the final seeds, and for new_supplier only a mismatched-seed "indicative only" estimate. That is not a basis for calling the adopted engine characterized. 4-4R closes it.
**Design: exactly the original 4-4's, with the adopted engine in place of the earlier governed one.**
- **All four scenarios** (baseline, new_supplier, period_shift, unpredictable_dissolution) as they are; **40 paired campaigns each** of 30 trials; **three strategies: the adopted engine (`recalibrated`), the same models without governance (ungoverned) and random recipes**, each given the identical lab day per seed.
- **Same measures and same verdict rule as 4-4:** the headline is the number of truly good recipes among the 30 trials (scored on the lab's noise-free truth in each trial's own context); the difference is the adopted engine minus the other strategy, campaign by campaign; the paired bootstrap 95% interval (2,000 resamples, the 4-4 bootstrap seed 20261014); "better" if the interval is wholly above 0, "worse" if wholly below, otherwise "no clear difference"; against the ungoverned engine and against random recipes, per scenario, never pooled; the same table, the same automatic "things that do not look good" section, the same stated-chance reliability and Brier tables, the same "what this does not show".
- **No new pass or fail bar. It is descriptive, as 4-4 was.** Whatever it shows is reported in full, including any scenario where the adopted engine still loses to the ungoverned one.
- **Seeds: 11500 to 11539 (40 seeds), reserved now.** Verified before use against every spent, reserved and sealed range in the seed log (10101 to 10104, 10200 to 10239, 10901 to 10905, 10930 to 10970, 11000 to 11039, 11100 to 11179, 11300 to 11339, 11400 to 11479, the retired 10300 to 10339, and the sealed 7,000,001 to 7,000,040 and 7,000,101 to 7,000,140): no overlap, no mention of any seed in 11500 to 11539 in any tracked file, and no stored run file named with one.
- **Two changes to the reporting code, made so that the report is honest for this engine, not to change any measure** (the defaults reproduce the original 4-4 report exactly, which `check_comparison.bat` keeps testing): (1) the report names the engine under test and the seed range from parameters instead of fixed words; (2) the adopted engine's decisions list as "stale" only the targets that still get the old response (a target R re-centres or finds working is not listed), so a count of "trials with a stale target" taken from the decisions would under-report how often the watch fired; the campaign records therefore also carry the watch's own flag and R's evidence class for each decision, and the report states, for this engine, how often the watch fired and how R classed what it found.
**Tool:** `tools/run_dev_comparison_r.py` (about 30 minutes; output in `runs/devcomparison_r/`, not in git).

### Seed ledger: two reserved blocks formally RETIRED (2026-10-12)
- **10300 to 10339: RETIRED.** It was reserved for the period_shift version 2 comparison; Finding A was closed as a finding about the lab and no harsher scenario is pursued, so it will never be used. It was never generated, run or examined.
- **11321 to 11339: RETIRED.** It was spare in the block reserved for the reshaped scenario; that scenario is closed with no redesign. Never used.
- A retired block is not available as a "fresh" block for any later test: it is dead, not spare. **Nothing in the seed log is left ambiguously "reserved"** except 11500 to 11539, reserved above and spent by 4-4R.

## Stage 4 Acceptance Readiness Summary (2026-10-12) [SYNTHETIC DATA]
**What this document is.** The single place that collects what is known about the adopted Stage 4 engine, with the 4-4R results in full and every open limitation carried forward as a disclosed, unresolved limitation, not re-investigated here. It is the basis for deciding whether and how to approach 4-5. **It contains no 4-5 design and makes no recommendation about it.** Everything is measured in the synthetic development lab; nothing says anything about real tablets or a client.

> **THE MOST IMPORTANT SENTENCE IN THIS SUMMARY: the adopted engine loses to simply ignoring the alarm in all four tested scenarios, confirmed by the full 4-4R comparison, not merely undemonstrated as better.**
>
> (Precisely: the comparison arm is the ungoverned engine, the same models with no governance at all, so it ignores the alarm and also has none of rules 1 to 4; the accounting in section 2 puts most of the remaining gap in the exploratory picks and bridging trials of rules 3 and 4 rather than in the alarm response. The measured fact stands as stated: in baseline, new_supplier, period_shift and unpredictable_dissolution the adopted engine's interval against it is wholly below 0.)

### 1. The engine being described
The adopted engine is `RecalibratedStrategy` (R) on top of the governed engine's rules 1 to 4, under the adoption scope recorded in "Adoption of R" above (the exact statement is also the constant `ADOPTED_SCOPE`, kept equal to the record by a check). It is covered by the engine lock together with `governed.py` and `baselines.py`; fingerprints are in "Adoption of R". Last full `check_all` before 4-4R: 947 passed, 0 failed, 1 old robot-reach warning; the final one is recorded at the end of this section.

### 2. 4-4R: the full results (seeds 11500 to 11539; 40 paired campaigns per scenario; three strategies; the original 4-4's measures, verdict rule and report; no pass or fail bar)
Raw report: `runs/devcomparison_r/summary.md` (not in git); tool `tools/run_dev_comparison_r.py`; 480 campaigns, 27 minutes of computing.

**Truly good recipes per 30-trial campaign (min to max), and the verdicts (the difference is the adopted engine minus the other strategy, paired; 95% bootstrap interval; "better" only if the interval is wholly above 0, "worse" only if wholly below).**

| Scenario | Adopted engine (R) | Ungoverned | Random | R vs ungoverned | R vs random |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| baseline | 29.6 (28 to 30) | 30.0 (30 to 30) | 1.6 (0 to 4) | **worse** (-0.4; -0.6 to -0.2; 0 wins, 26 ties, 14 losses) | **better** (+28.0; +27.6 to +28.4; 40/0/0) |
| new_supplier | 28.4 (26 to 30) | 30.0 (30 to 30) | 0.9 (0 to 3) | **worse** (-1.6; -1.9 to -1.2; 0 wins, 9 ties, 31 losses) | **better** (+27.5; +27.1 to +27.9; 40/0/0) |
| period_shift | 28.4 (26 to 30) | 30.0 (30 to 30) | 0.9 (0 to 3) | **worse** (-1.6; -1.9 to -1.3; 0 wins, 3 ties, 37 losses) | **better** (+27.4; +27.0 to +27.9; 40/0/0) |
| unpredictable_dissolution | 6.0 (2 to 11) | 9.2 (4 to 16) | 1.4 (0 to 4) | **worse** (-3.2; -4.3 to -2.1; 5 wins, 5 ties, 30 losses) | **better** (+4.5; +3.8 to +5.3; 38 wins, 1 tie, 1 loss) |

**Plain reading: the adopted engine still loses to the same models without governance in all four scenarios, by the rule fixed in advance, and beats random recipes in all four.** After the event, new_supplier (from trial 8) is 21.4 against 23.0 (-1.6; -1.9 to -1.2, worse) and period_shift (from trial 6) is 23.4 against 25.0 (-1.6; -1.9 to -1.3, worse).
**Against the earlier engine's 4-4 (seeds 10200 to 10239; a different set of seeds, so compared but NOT pooled or paired):** R minus ungoverned was -0.4, -1.6, -1.6 and -3.2 (baseline, new_supplier, period_shift, unpredictable_dissolution); the earlier engine's was -0.5, -3.8, -14.0 and -3.5. The gap shrank a great deal in period_shift (-14.0 to -1.6), more than halved in new_supplier (-3.8 to -1.6), and did not change in baseline or unpredictable_dissolution (the intervals overlap). This matches the partial test's period_shift figure (-1.46).

**Where the remaining gap sits (descriptive accounting from the stored campaigns; R's not-good trials per campaign by pick kind; the ungoverned engine had none in the first three scenarios).**

| Scenario | R not good per campaign | By kind |
|---|---|---|
| baseline | 0.38 | exploratory 0.35 (of 6.0 picks); stale exploration 0.03 (of 0.1) |
| new_supplier | 1.55 | bridging 1.35 (of 3.0 picks); exploratory 0.20 (of 5.0) |
| period_shift | 1.62 | exploratory 1.60 (of 6.0 picks); stale exploration 0.03 (of 0.1) |
| unpredictable_dissolution | 24.02 (ungoverned 20.80) | space filling 9.62 (of 11.9); local search 9.40 (of 11.9); exploratory 4.88 (of 6.0); stale exploration 0.12 (of 0.2) |

**So, after the adoption, nearly all of the remaining gap in the first three scenarios is in rules 3 and 4** (the exploratory picks, capped at one in five trials, and the three bridging trials), **not in the watch**; in unpredictable_dissolution it is rules 1 and 2 (dissolution is not modelled, so the engine searches without it). These are the engine's designed prices of caution, in a lab where the ungoverned engine's picks happen to be fine; the 4-4R data say how much that caution costs here.

**What the watch and R did.** The watch fired in 22 of 40 campaigns (baseline), 27 (new_supplier), 40 (period_shift) and 19 (unpredictable_dissolution). At the stale decisions R classed the evidence (per stale target per decision) as: baseline offset 69, fine 44, mixed 3; new_supplier offset 84, fine 177, mixed 1; period_shift offset 762, fine 285, mixed 3; unpredictable_dissolution offset 49, fine 84, mixed 9. **The promise check sent no decision back to the earlier response in any scenario** (it has never been triggered in any test).

> **NOTE ON "STALE" COUNTS: the 4-4 and 4-4R reports do NOT count the same population of trials as "stale", and the two numbers must not be compared directly without this note.** In the original 4-4 report (the earlier engine) every target the watch flagged was handed to the earlier response, so "trials with a stale target" was both the trials the watch flagged and the trials that got the earlier response (one number). The adopted engine **removes the targets it classes OFFSET (re-centres) and FINE (finds working) from that bucket before the earlier response is ever applied to them**, so for it the two are different numbers. The 4-4R report therefore shows both, and its decisions' own `stale` field counts only the second. Per campaign, on average:
>
> | Scenario | 4-4, earlier engine: trials with a stale target (flagged AND earlier response) | 4-4R, adopted engine: trials with a watch-flagged target | 4-4R: of those, trials in which a target still got the earlier response |
> |---|---|---|---|
> | baseline | 2.2 | 2.9 | 0.1 |
> | new_supplier | 6.5 | 5.3 | 0.0 |
> | period_shift | 17.6 | 13.2 | 0.1 |
> | unpredictable_dissolution | 2.1 | 3.1 | 0.2 |
>
> The like-for-like comparison of "how often did the watch fire" is the first two number columns (the earlier engine's single number against the adopted engine's watch-flagged number); the last column is the adopted engine's use of the earlier response, which has no counterpart in the earlier engine's single number. Any statement in this document or elsewhere that sets a 4-4 stale-trial figure beside a 4-4R one refers to this note.

**Stated chances (the same tables as in 4-4).** The adopted engine's Brier score is higher (less honest by that measure) than the ungoverned engine's for every target (dissolution, hardness, friability and all targets together) in baseline, new_supplier and period_shift. In unpredictable_dissolution it states no chance for dissolution or for all targets together, and its friability and hardness scores are no worse than the ungoverned engine's (0.000 against 0.001, and 0.000 against 0.006). As in 4-4 this compares scores on different mixes of trials (the adopted engine states chances on harder trials, the ungoverned engine states about 1.0 on picks that are all good), so it is weak evidence of dishonesty; the absolute scores are small (0.000 to 0.031). **A real caveat to the adoption scope's claim that chances stay honest:** in period_shift the adopted engine's stated chance of meeting every target fell in the 60% to 80% bin 33 times and was truly good only 36% of the time (mean stated 0.71), and in the 20% to 40% and 40% to 60% bins 50% of 6 and 56% of 9; the main bin (80% to 100%, 1,135 decisions) was truly good 98% of the time (mean stated 0.99). The few intermediate chances are overconfident or noisy at these sample sizes.

### 3. Disclosed, unresolved limitations (carried forward as they stand; none re-investigated here)
1. **The false-alarm rate of the watch (about 40% of unshifted campaigns).** The stated 90% ranges are hit about 82% of the time for hardness (and misses cluster, which the plan's independence assumption does not allow for). In 4-4R the watch fired in 22 of 40 baseline campaigns, 27 of 40 in new_supplier and 19 of 40 in unpredictable_dissolution. R softens what a false alarm costs; it does not change how often the watch fires. (Stale counts: see the note on "stale" counts above.)
2. **The late-block pattern in R's not-good trials: it REAPPEARED in 4-4R, as the adoption record said to flag.** period_shift, not-good trials per campaign by block of trials 6 to 12, 13 to 18, 19 to 24, 25 to 30: **0.15, 0.12, 0.40, 0.95** (the partial test: 0.12, 0.26, 0.85 for the last three). A smaller rise appears in the baseline (0.00, 0.00, 0.10, 0.28) and not in new_supplier after its bridging trials (1.45, 0.00, 0.07, 0.03). In period_shift almost all of R's not-good trials are exploratory (rule 3) picks (1.60 of 1.62 per campaign), but **whether the late rise is made of those picks, and why it rises, was not looked at and is unexplained.** It is the reason R trails the ungoverned engine in period_shift by 1.6.
3. **The new_supplier gain is unexplained.** R's gap to the ungoverned engine in new_supplier is -1.6 here against -3.8 for the earlier engine (different seeds), consistent with the +2.92 seen in the partial test; no reason for it is known.
4. **Reshaping (ranking-breaking) shifts are untested.** No scenario of that kind has been found that is both ranking-breaking and winnable (the named observation above); R has never faced one; the promise check, which exists for that case, has never fired.
5. **R does not beat simply ignoring the alarm; this is now a measured fact, not an open question (see the labeled sentence at the top of this summary).** The ungoverned engine, which ignores every alarm, beats R in all four scenarios of 4-4R. R's value is honesty about stated ranges and a far smaller cost than the earlier response, not recipes.
6. **The residual cost of governance by design (new in 4-4R's accounting).** The remaining gap to the ungoverned engine is in the exploratory picks (about 6 per campaign, the cap), the bridging trials (3 per new_supplier campaign; 55% truly good in 4-4R, 60% in 4-4) and the handling of an unmodelled dissolution. **Five constants remain provisional and revisitable under the standing rule (the exploratory cap of 1 in 5, 3 bridging trials, the top 20% share, the refit interval and the candidate count; "after seeing the 4-4 comparison, never after the 4-5 run").** Whether to revisit any of them is a decision for the project owner; none is proposed here.
7. **unpredictable_dissolution is unchanged by the adoption** (-3.2 here, -3.5 in 4-4): R acts only on a stale target, and dissolution is not modelled there.
8. **The intermediate stated chances are not reliably honest** (see the caveat under "Stated chances").
9. **The lab's limits:** one smooth formula family; the engine and the lab were developed together on these four scenarios, so every figure here is a development comparison, not a blind test.
10. **The sealed acceptance lab (version 2) is blind to its seeds but is not a fully blind acceptance test:** its shape constants were adjusted twice by someone aware of Stage 3's findings, and the permanent caveat in the acceptance report says so.
11. **The Stage 3 findings that frame all of this still hold:** on real batch data dissolution is barely predictable, the warnings are the platform's safeguard and are weak for a new supplier or period, and nothing has been run on client data.

### 4. Seed ledger (final for Stage 4 so far)
**Spent:** 10200 to 10239 (4-4; period_shift governed replayed once to recover recipes, result not re-judged); 10930 to 10970 (Finding B, the step calibration); 11000 to 11039 and 11100 to 11179 (the all-stale tests); 11300 to 11320 (the reshaped calibration); 11400 to 11479 (the partial recalibration test); **11500 to 11539 (4-4R)**. **Retired, never used:** 10300 to 10339 and 11321 to 11339. **Sealed:** 7,000,101 to 7,000,140 live, 7,000,001 to 7,000,040 retired. **Nothing is reserved.** Nothing in a spent block may be used to judge a later engine change.

**Final full `check_all` for this summary (after 4-4R and the report changes): 948 passed, 0 failed, 1 old robot-reach warning (all 20 checks, 16 min 28 s).**

## Stages 5 to 7 (planner and executor, configurable cell simulator, 3D replay viewer): the build plan, decided on 2026-10-12 [SYNTHETIC / ILLUSTRATIVE]
**Authorisation.** The project owner asked for Stages 5, 6 and 7 of the approved platform plan to be built autonomously, with one report at the end of Stage 7. **4-5 stays gated and is not touched; Stage 8 and later are not started.** Standing rules apply throughout: the proof of concept and `main` are unchanged; every stage ends with a health check and a commit; every file is labelled illustrative or synthetic; nothing needs the internet at run time; no existing check is weakened.
**Order: the planner and the executor first, then the configurable simulator, then the viewer.** The plan lists the simulator (Stage 5) before the planner and executor (Stage 6), but it also says the platform's fault timing "is taken from the executor, not from the proof of concept's rule". The simulator therefore cannot be built first without a stand-in for something that is about to be built. The stages keep their numbers, their health checks and their commits; only the build order of 5 and 6 is swapped.
**New parts and how they may import (the architecture guard is extended, never loosened).** `planner` (turns a recipe, the setup and the layout into a motion plan; imports nothing else), `executor` (runs a plan and returns the executed timeline; may import `planner`), `platform_cell` (the configurable simulator; may import `contracts`, `settings`, `cell_simulator`, `planner`, `executor`), `viewer` (writes the 3D replay file; reads data files only), and a supporting `platform_runner` (wires them, like `runner`). No existing part's list of allowed imports changes.
**The simulator reuses the proof of concept's own simulator rather than copying it.** `platform_cell` builds the proof of concept's settings from the setup with the existing converter, subclasses the proof of concept's simulation class and replaces only how a trial is run (the plan, the executor's timeline, the faults) and how the next trial is fitted. Everything else (the clock, the staffing calendar, events, alerts, handovers, snapshots, the run record) is the proof of concept's, so a platform run is a normal run record that the existing dashboard can read. **No proof of concept file is edited.** Consequence, stated plainly: a setup is simulated only if the converter can represent it (the proof of concept's five ingredients and its two targets); any other setup is refused with the converter's own plain notes. Setups beyond that need a lab and an engine for them, which is later work.
**What changes relative to the proof of concept, by design (all from the plan).** Faults come from the setup, scripted AND random; a fault costs what its recovery actions cost (tap and shake, put the bottle back, purge, pick it up again, retry the rest), not "a whole step, a purge and a whole step again"; a retry that fails skips the candidate, and cleaning still follows when the setup says so. The proof of concept's rule stays in the proof of concept.
**Known-answer anchors written before the code.** (1) Run on the hand-authored example plan and its scripted jam, the executor must reproduce the hand-authored executed timeline action by action (ids, origins, types, outcomes, actual start and duration, recovery ids, the step totals 1,800 s planned and 5,635 s actual for dispense). (2) Every plan the planner generates must pass the same format rules the examples pass (step totals within 1 s, every wait with a named reason, friability steps only when the setup needs them, picks match places, no gaps or overlaps), and the JSON schemas. (3) The simulator's trial count must match an independent recount from the setup's own numbers when a timing or a fault is changed.
**The 3D library.** The plan requires the viewer to bundle its 3D library. `three.js` r128 (MIT licence, the version the reference viewer already uses) is copied once into the project from its public address; its licence text is kept beside it. No Python library is added. The font already bundled in `static/` is embedded. The viewer has no logic of its own: it replays an executed timeline, takes the moment to show from the address (`trial` and `t`), and says so if WebGL is unavailable.
**Verification without reading code.** Double-click `make_platform_demo.bat` (builds a platform run from the saved example setup and opens the 3D replay of trial 8's jam), then the new checks: `check_planner.bat`, `check_executor.bat`, `check_cellsim.bat`, `check_viewer.bat` (the last drives Chrome headless on a real page and looks at the picture).

## Stages 5 to 8 as built (2026-10-13) [SYNTHETIC / ILLUSTRATIVE]
**Authorisation.** The project owner extended the mandate on 2026-10-13: complete Stages 5 to 8 without waiting for reports, and then also the design document for 4-5 (design only), Stages 9 and 10, every check the whole project needs, a demo script and a plain-language explanation of the system, "taking the best decision wherever required". **What this did NOT authorise, and what was not done:** running the sealed acceptance run (4-5), any change to rules 3 or 4, any change to the locked engine files (`governed.py`, `baselines.py`, `recalibrated.py`), any use of the sealed lab, any change of a seed after seeing outcomes, any merge into `main`.
**What was built and how it was checked.** Stage 6 (planner and executor): `check_planner.bat` 21 checks, `check_executor.bat` 20 checks. Stage 5 (configurable cell simulator): `check_cellsim.bat` 21 checks. Stage 7 (3D replay viewer): `check_viewer.bat` 24 checks, driving real headless Chrome. Stage 8 (dashboard integration): `check_dashboard.bat` 30 checks.
**Decisions taken without asking, each with its reason (change any of them by saying so).**
1. *The planner can rescale a recipe to a batch, and says so in the plan.* The proof of concept's five ingredient levels add up to about 77%, not 100% (a limitation already stated on its last page). The planner would refuse such a recipe; rather than change the proof of concept's lab, the planner gained an explicit `rescale` option, used only by the configurable simulator, which scales the five levels to the batch and writes that fact into the plan's reason line. Plans for a recipe that really adds up are unchanged.
2. *A fault costs what its recovery actions cost.* In a platform run the binder jam of trial 8 costs about 64 minutes (tap and shake, put the bottle back, purge, pick it up again, retry), not the proof of concept's 90. This is by design (it was the point of building the executor first) and is not tuned. It also means a platform run fits more trials in the same window than the proof of concept does; the figure a run gives is what its setup produced, not a target.
3. *The dashboard gains two pages only when the record is from a platform run.* The run record has one new optional section, `platform` (plain text lines written in advance by `cellpoc/platform_info.py`). A proof-of-concept record has no such section and the dashboard shows exactly its six pages as before: this is proved by recording the dashboard's output on the demo record before the change (the number of text blocks, figures, buttons, charts and tables and a fingerprint of every word) and comparing after. The store was changed in one small way: a missing optional section (default `None`) is read as absent instead of making the file "out of date"; every other missing field still does, and the damaged-record message is unchanged (Stage 5's check passes).
4. *The text for the new pages is worked out in the platform runner, not the dashboard.* The dashboard still calculates nothing: the guard (`checks/architecture.py`) caught a path join written with `/` in the first draft of the replay page (counted as arithmetic) and the page was rewritten with `joinpath`; the guard itself is unchanged in what it forbids.
5. *The architecture guard lists one new supporting part, `platform_info`* (may import `contracts`, `ingest`, `modelling`, `planner`, `platform_cell`; no network), and `platform_runner` may import it. No existing part's list changed.
6. *The engine in a platform run is the proof of concept's adaptive strategy, not the Stage 4 governed engine.* Wiring the governed engine in would change what a run produces and is tied to the unresolved questions about rules 3 and 4 and to the gated acceptance run. The 'Data and model' page says so and repeats the measured finding about the adopted engine (it loses to simply ignoring the alarm in all four tested scenarios).
7. *`checks/cellsim_check.py` no longer lists the whole `cellpoc/dashboard` folder as untouched.* Stage 8 changed `cellpoc/dashboard/app.py` on purpose and added two page files; the eight proof-of-concept page files and the root `app.py` are still listed as untouched, and the Stage 8 check proves the proof of concept renders as before. No check was removed or weakened.
**Known limit of the checks.** The dashboard page that holds the 3D replay was verified in the test harness (the page embeds exactly the saved replay file, character for character) and the replay file itself is verified in real Chrome by `check_viewer.bat`; a picture of the finished dashboard page could not be taken automatically here (Streamlit loads its content over a live connection, which the headless screenshot did not wait for). The by-eye step is: double-click `run_platform.bat`, open the "3D replay" page and press Play.

## Stages 9 and 10 as built (2026-10-13) [SYNTHETIC / ILLUSTRATIVE]
**Authorisation.** The same instruction (2026-10-13): also do Stages 9 and 10 and every check the whole project needs, then a demo script and a plain-language explanation. **Added the same day: "everything should be with the Deloitte template or branding"**, applied to every file the platform writes for people to read.
**Decisions, each with its reason.**
1. *Reports are written from the run record only* (new part `reports`, allowed to import `contracts` and `run_store`, no network), so every number matches the dashboard's. Files: a branded report page, a PDF, an Excel workbook, three CSV tables. No clock time is written, so the same record gives the same files.
2. *The PDF is printed by an installed browser (Chrome or Edge), not by a new library.* CLAUDE.md forbids a new library without approval; a PDF library was not needed. If no browser is found the report page says to print to PDF itself. Excel uses `openpyxl`, which the project already uses and pins.
